The Man of Mode, or Sir Fopling Flutter

George Etherege — EMOTHE0449_TheManOfModeOrSirFoplingFlutter

DRAMATIS PERSONÆ

  • Mr. DORIMANT, }
  • Mr. MEDLEY, }
  • YOUNG BELLAIR, }
  • SIR FOPLING FLUTTER, } Gentlemen
  • LADY TOWNLEY, }
  • EMILIA, }
  • Mrs. LOVEIT, }
  • BELINDA, }
  • LADY WOODVIL, }
  • HARRIET, her daughter } Gentlemwomen
  • PERT, }
  • BUSY, } waiting-women
  • A Shoemaker
  • An Orange-Woman
  • Three Slovenly Bullies
  • Two Chairmen
  • Mr. SMIRK, a parson
  • HANDY, a valet-de-chambre
  • Pages, Footmen, etc.

Prologue

BY SIR CAR SCROOPE, BARONET

1Like dancers on the ropes poor poets fare,
Most perish young, the rest in danger are;
This, one would think, should make our authors wary,
But, gamester like, the giddy fools miscarry.
A lucky hand or two so tempts 'em on,
They cannot leave off play till they're undone.
With modest fears a muse does first begin,
Like a young wench newly enticed to sin;
But tickled once with praise, by her good will,
The wanton fool would never more lie still.
'Tis an old mistress you'll meet here to-night,
Whose charms you once have look'd on with delight;
But now of late such dirty drabs have known ye,
A muse o' th' better sort's ashamed to own ye.
Nature well drawn, and wit, must now give place
To gaudy nonsense and to dull grimace:
Nor is it strange that you should like so much
That kind of wit, for most of yours is such.
But I'm afraid that while to France we go,
To bring you home fine dresses, dance, and show,
The stage, like you, will but more foppish grow.
Of foreign wares why should we fetch the scum
When we can be so richly served at home?
For, Heav'n be thank'd, 'tis not so wise an age
But your own follies may supply the stage.
Though often plough'd, there's no great fear the soil
Should barren grow by the too frequent toil,
While at your doors are to be daily found
Such loads of dunghill to manure the ground.
'Tis by your follies that we players thrive,
As the physicians by diseases live;
And as each year some new distemper reigns,
Whose friendly poison helps t'increase their gains
So among you there starts up every day
Some new unheard-of fool for us to play.
Then for your own sakes be not too severe,
Nor what you all admire at home, damn here:
Since each is fond of his own ugly face,
Why should you, when we hold it, break the glass?

ACT I

SCENE I. –

A Dressing-room. A table covered with a toilet; clothes laid ready.

Enter DORIMANT in his gown and slippers, with a note in his hand made up, repeating verses.

Dorimant

2Now for some ages had the pride of Spain
Made the sun shine on half the world in vain.

[Then looking on the note.

[For Mrs. LOVEIT.]

What a dull insipid thing is a billet-doux written in cold blood, after the heat of the business is over! It is a tax upon good-nature which I have here been labouring to pay, and have done it, but with as much regret as ever fanatic paid the Royal Aid or Church Duties, 'Twill have the same fate, I know, that all my notes to her have had of late, 'twill not be thought kind enough. Faith, women are i' the right when they jealousy examine our letters, for in them we always first discover our decay of passion. –Hey! Who waits?

Enter HANDY

Handy

3Sir –

Dorimant

4Call a footman.

Handy

5None of 'em are come yet.

Dorimant

6Dogs! Will they ever lie snoring a-bed till noon?

Handy

7'Tis all one, sir: if they 're up, you indulge 'em so they're ever poaching after whores all the morning.

Dorimant

8Take notice henceforward, who's wanting in his duty, the next clap he gets, he shall rot for an example. What vermin are those chattering without?

Handy

9Foggy Nan the orange-woman and swearing Tom the shoemaker.

Dorimant

10Go; call in that overgrown jade with the flasket of guts before her; fruit is refreshing in a morning.

[Exit HANDY.

It is not that I love you less
Than when before your feet I lay.

Enter ORANGE-WOMAN with HANDY.

How now, Double Tripe! what news do you bring?

Orange-Woman

11News! Here's the best fruit has come to town t'year; gad, I was up before four a'clock this morning, and bought all the choice i' the market.

Dorimant

12The nasty refuse of your shop.

Orange-Woman

13You need not make mouths at it; I assure you 'tis all culled ware.

Dorimant

14The citizens buy better on a holiday in their walk to Totnam.

Orange-Woman

15Good or bad, tis all one; I never knew you commend anything. Lord! would the ladies had heard you talk of 'em as I have done. Here, bid you man give me an angel.

[Sets down the fruit.

Dorimant

16Give the bawd her fruit again.

Orange-Woman

17Well, on my conscience, there never was the like of you. God's my life, I had almost forgot to tell you there is a young gentlewoman lately come to town with her mother, that is so taken with you.

Dorimant

18Is she handsome?

Orange-Woman

19Nay, gad, there are few finer women, I tell you but so, and a hugeous fortune, they say. Here, eat this peach, it comes from the stone; 'tis better than any Newington y' have tasted.

Dorimant

20This fine woman, I'll lay my life,
[Taking the peach.] is some awkward, ill-fashioned, country toad, who, not having above four dozen of black hairs on her head, has adorned her baldness with a large white fruz, that she may look sparkishly in the forefront of the King's box at an old play.

Orange-Woman

21Gad, you'd change your note quickly if you did but see her.

Dorimant

22How came she to know me?

Orange-Woman

23She saw you yesterday at the Change; she told me you came and fooled with the woman at the next shop.

Dorimant

24I remember there was a mask observed me indeed. Fooled, did she say?

Orange-Woman

25Ay, I vow she told me twenty things you said too; and acted with her head and with her body so like you –

Enter MEDLEY.

Medley

26Dorimant, my life, my joy, my darling sin, how dost tou?

Orange-Woman

27Lord! what a filthy trick these men have got of kissing one another!

[She spits.

Medley

28Why do you suffer this cartload of scandal to come near you and make your neighbours think you so improvident to need a bawd?

Orange-Woman

29Good, now we shall have it! you did but want him to help you; come, pay me for my fruit.

Medley

30Make us thankful for it, huswife; bawds are as much out of fashion as gentlemen-ushers: none but old formal ladies use the one, and none but foppish old stagers employ the other – go, you are an insignificant brandy bottle.

Dorimant

31Nay, there are wrong her, three quarts of canary is her business.

Orange-Woman

32What you please, gentlemen.

Dorimant

33To him! give him as good as he brings.

Orange-Woman

34Hang him, there is not such another heathen in the town again, except it be the shoemaker without.

Medley

35I shall see you hold up your hand at the bar next sessions for murder, huswife; that shoemaker can take his oath you are in fee with the doctor's to sell green fruit to the gentry, that the crudities may breed diseases.

Orange-Woman

36Pray give me my money.

Dorimant

37Not a penny; when you bring the gentlewoman hither you spoke of, you shall be paid.

Orange-Woman

38The gentlewoman! the gentlewoman may be as honest as your sisters, for aught as I know. Pray pay me, Mr. Dorimant, and do not abuse me so; I have an honester way of living, you know it.

Medley

39Was there ever such a resty bawd?

Dorimant

40Some jade's tricks she has, but she makes amends when she's in good-humour. Come, tell me the lady's name, and Handy shall pay you.

Orange-Woman

41I must not, she forbid me.

Dorimant

42That's a sure sign she would have you.

Medley

43Where does she live?

Orange-Woman

44They lodge at my house.

Medley

45Nay, then she's in a hopeful way.

Orange-Woman

46Good Mr. Medley, say your pleasure of me, but take heed how you affront my house. God's my life, in a hopeful way!

Dorimant

47Prithee, peace! what kind of woman's the mother?

Orange-Woman

48A goodly grave gentlewoman. Lord! how she talks against the wild young men o' the town! As for your part, she thinks you an arrant devil; should she see you, on my conscience she would look if you had not a cloven foot.

Dorimant

49Does she know me?

Orange-Woman

50Only by hearsay; a thousand horrid stories have been told her of you, and she believes 'em all.

Medley

51By the character, this should be the famous Lady Woodvil and her daughter Harriet.

Orange-Woman

52The devil's in him for guessing, I think.

Dorimant

53Do you know 'em?

Medley

54Both very well; the mother's a great admirer of the forms and civility of the last age.

Dorimant

55An antiquated beauty may be allowed to be out of humour at the freedoms of the present. This is a good account of the mother; pray, what is the daughter?

Medley

56Why, first she's an heiress, vastly rich.

Dorimant

57And handsome?

Medley

58What alteration a twelvemonth may have bred in her I know not, but a year ago she was the beautifullest creature I ever saw; a fine, easy, clean shape; light brown hair in abundance; her features regular; her complexion clear and lively; large wanton eyes; but above all, a mouth that has made me kiss it a thousand times in imagination, teeth white and even, and pretty pouting lips, with a little moisture ever hanging on them, that look like the Provence rose fresh on the bush, ere the morning sun has quite drawn up the dew.

Dorimant

59Rapture, mere rapture!

Orange-Woman

60Nay, gad, he tells you true; she's a delicate creature.

Dorimant

61Has she wit?

Medley

62More than is usual in her sex, and as much malice. Then she's as wild as you would wish her, and has a demureness in her looks that makes it so surprising.

Dorimant

63Flesh and blood cannot hear this, and not long to know her.

Medley

64I wonder what makes her mother bring her up to town; an old doting keeper cannot be more jealous of his mistress.

Orange-Woman

65She made me laugh yesterday; there was a judge came to visit 'em, and the old man, she told me, did so stare upon her, and when he saluted her smacked so heartily; who would think it of 'em?

Medley

66God a mercy, a judge!

Dorimant

67Do 'em right, the gentlemen of the long robe have not been wanting by their good examples to countenance the crying sin o' the nation.

Medley

68Come, on with your trappings; 'tis later than you imagine.

Dorimant

69Call in the shoemaker, Handy.

Orange-Woman

70Good Mr. Dorimant, pay me; gad, I had rather give you my fruit than stay to be abused by that foul-mouthed rogue; what you gentlemen say, it matters not much, but such a dirty fellow does one more disgrace.

Dorimant

71Give her ten shillings, and be sure you tell the young gentlewoman I must be acquainted with her.

Orange-Woman

72Now do you long to be tempting this pretty creature. Well, heavens mend you!

Medley

73Farewell, Boga. [Exeunt ORANGE-WOMAN and HANDY.]
Dorimant, when did you see your pis-aller, as you call her Mrs. Loveit?

Dorimant

74Not these two days.

Medley

75And how stand affairs between you?

Dorimant

76There has been great patching of late, much ado; we make a shift to hang together.

Medley

77I wonder how her mighty spirit bears it.

Dorimant

78I'll enough, on all conscience; I never knew so violent a creature.

Medley

79She's the most passionate in her love, and the most extravagant in her jealousy, of any woman I ever heard of. What note is that?

Dorimant

80An excuse I am going to send her for the neglect I am guilty of.

Medley

81Prithee read it.

Dorimant

82No; but if you will take the pains you may.

Medley

83[reads] "I never was a lover of business, but now I have a just reason to hate it, since it has kept me these two days from seeing you. I intend to wait upon you in the afternoon, and in the pleasure of your conversation forget all I have suffered during this tedious absence." This business of yours, Dorimant, has been with a vizard at the playhouse; I have had an eye on you. If some malicious body should betray you, this kind note would hardly make your peace with her.

Dorimant

84I desire no better.

Medley

85Why, would her knowledge of it oblige you?

Dorimant

86Most infinitely; next to the coming to a good understanding with a new mistress, I love a quarrel with and old one; but the devil's in't, there has been such a calm in my affairs of late, I have not had the pleasure of making a woman so much as break her fan, to be sullen, or forswear herself these three days.

Medley

87A very great misfortune. Let me see, I love mischief well enough to forward this business myself; I'll about it presently and though I know the truth of what you've done will set her a-raving, I'll heighten it a little with invention, leave her in a fit o' the mother, and be here again before you're ready.

Dorimant

88Pray stay; you may spare yourself the labour; the business is undertaken already by one who will manage it with as much address, and I think with a little more malice than you can.

Medley

89Who i' the devil's name can this be?

Dorimant

90Why the vizard –that very vizard you saw me with.

Medley

91Does she love mischief so well as to betray herself to spite another?

Dorimant

92Not so neither, Medley. I will make you comprehend the mystery: this mask, for a farther confirmation of what I have been these two days swearing to her, made me yesterday at the playhouse make her a promise before her face utterly to break off with Loveit; and because she tenders my reputation, and would not have me do a barbarous thing, has contrived a way to give me a handsome occasion.

Medley

93Very good.

Dorimant

94She intends, about an hour before me, this afternoon to make Loveit a visit, and (having the privilege, by reason of a professed friendship between 'em) to talk of her concerns.

Medley

95Is she a friend?

Dorimant

96Oh, an intimate friend!

Medley

97Better and better; pray proceed.

Dorimant

98She means insensibly to insinuate a discourse of me, and artificially raise her jealousy to such a height, that transported with the first motions of her passion, she shall fly upon me with all the fury imaginable as soon as ever I enter; the quarrel being thus happily begun, I am to play my art, confess and justify all my roguery, swear her impertinence and ill-humour makes her intolerable, tax her with the next fop that comes into my head, and in a huff march away; slight her, and leave her to be taken by whosoever thinks it worth his time to lie down before her.

Medley

99This vizard is a spark, and has a genius that makes her worthy of yourself, Dorimant.

Enter HANDY, Shoemaker, and Footman.

Dorimant

100You rogue there, who sneak like a dog that has flung down a dish, if you do not mend your waiting I'll uncase you, and turn you loose to the wheel of fortune. Handy, seal this, and let him run with it presently.

[Exeunt HANDY and Footman.

Medley

101Since you're resolved on a quarrel, why do you send her this kind note?

Dorimant

102To keep her at home in order to the business.
[To the Shoemaker.] How now, you drunken sot?

Shoemaker

103'Zbud, you have no reason to talk; I have not had a bottle of sack of yours in my belly this fortnight.

Medley

104The orange-woman says your neighbours take notice what a heathen you are, and design to inform the bishop and have you burned for an atheist.

Shoemaker

105Damn her, dunghill! if her husband does not remove her, she stinks so the parish intend to indict him for a nuisance.

Medley

106I advise you like a friend, reform your life; you have brought the envy of the world upon you by living above yourself. Whoring and swearing are vices too genteel for a shoemaker.

Shoemaker

107'Zbud, I think you men of quality will grow as unreasonable as the women; you would engross the sins o' the nation; poor folks can no sooner be wicked, but they're railed at by their betters.

Dorimant

108Sirrah, I'll have you stand i' the pillory for this libel.

Shoemaker

109Some of you deserve it, I'm sure; there are so many of 'em, that our journeymen nowadays, instead of harmless ballads, sin nothing but your damned lampoons.

Dorimant

110Our lampoons, you rogue?

Shoemaker

111Nay, good master, why should not you write your own commentaries as well as Cæsar?

Medley

112The rascal's read, I perceive.

Shoemaker

113You know the old proverb –ale and history.

Dorimant

114Draw on my shoes, sirrah.

Shoemaker

115Here's a shoe.

Dorimant

116Sits with more wrinkles than there are in an angry bully's forehead.

Shoemaker

117'Zbud, as smooth as your mistress's skin does upon her; so strike your foot in home. 'Zbud, if e'er a monsieur of 'em all make more fashionable wear, I'll be content to have my ears whipped off with my own paring-knife.

Medley

118And served up in a ragout instead of coxcombs to a company of French shoemakers for a collation.

Shoemaker

119Hold, hold! damn 'em, caterpillars! let 'em feed upon cabbage. Come, master, your health this morning next my heart now.

Dorimant

120Go, get you home, and govern your family better; do not let your wife follow you to the alehouse, beat your whore, and lead you home in triumph.

Shoemaker

121'Zbud, there's never a man i' the town lives more like a gentleman with his wife than I do. I never mind her motions, she never inquires into mine; we speak to one another civilly, hate one another heartily, and because 'tis vulgar to lie and soak together, we have each of us our several settle-bed.

Dorimant

122Give him half-a-crown.

Medley

123Not without he will promise to be bloody drunk.

Shoemaker

124Tope's the word i' the eye of the world, for my master's honour, Robin.

Dorimant

125Do not debauch my servants, sirrah.

Shoemaker

126I only tip him the wink; he knows an alehouse from a hovel.

[Exit Shoemaker.

Dorimant

127My clothes, quickly.

Medley

128Where shall we dine to-day?

Enter BELLAIR.

Dorimant

129Where you will; here comes a good third man.

Bellair

130Your servant, gentlemen.

Medley

131Gentle sir, how will you answer this visit to your honourable mistress? 'Tis not her interest you should keep company with men of sense, who will be talking reason.

Bellair

132I do not fear her pardon, do you but grant me yours for my neglect of late.

Medley

133Though you've made us miserable by the want of your good company, to show you I am free from all resentment, may the beautiful cause of our misfortune give you all the joys happy lovers have shared ever since the world began.

Bellair

134You wish me in Heaven, but you believe me on my journey to Hell.

Medley

135You have a good strong faith, and that may contribute much towards your salvation. I confess I am but of an untoward constitution, apt to have doubts and scruples, and in love they are no less distracting than in religion; were I so near marriage, I should cry out by fits as I ride in my coach, Cuckold, Cuckold, with no less fury than the mad fanatic does Glory in Bethlem.

Bellair

136Because religion makes some run mad, must I live an atheist?

Medley

137Is it not great indiscretion for a man of credit, who may have money enough on his word, to go and deal with Jews who for little sums make men enter into bonds and give judgments?

Bellair

138Preach no more on this text, I am determined, and there is no hope of my conversion.

Dorimant

139[to HANDY, who is fiddling about him]. Leave, your unnecessary fiddling; a wasp that's buzzing about a man's nose at dinner is not more troublesome than thou art.

Handy

140You love to have your clothes hang just, sir.

Dorimant

141I love to be well dressed, sir; and think it no scandal to my understanding.

Handy

142Will you use the essence, or orange-flower water?

Dorimant

143I will smell as I do to-day, no offence to the ladies' noses.

Handy

144Your pleasure, sir.

Dorimant

145That a man's excellency should lie in neatly tying of a ribbon or a cravat! How careful's nature in furnishing the world with necessary coxcombs?

Bellair

146That's a mighty pretty suit of yours, Dorimant.

Dorimant

147I am glad't has your approbation.

Bellair

148No man in town has a better fancy in his clothes than you have.

Dorimant

149You will make me have an opinion of my genius.

Medley

150There is a great critic, I hear, in these matters lately arrived piping hot from Paris.

Bellair

151Sir Fopling Flutter, you mean.

Medley

152The same.

Bellair

153He thinks himself the pattern of modern gallantry.

Dorimant

154He is indeed the pattern of modern foppery.

Medley

155He was yesterday at the play, with a pair of gloves up to his elbows and a periwig more exactly curled than a lady's head newly dressed for a ball.

Bellair

156What a pretty lisp he has!

Dorimant

157Ho! that he affects in imitation of the people of quality of France.

Medley

158His head stands for the most part on one side, and his looks are more languishing than a lady's when she lolls at stretch in her coach or leans her head carelessly against the side of a box i' the playhouse.

Dorimant

159He is a person indeed of great acquired follies.

Medley

160He is like many others, beholding to his education for making him so eminent a coxcomb; many a fool had been lost to the world had their indulgent parents wisely bestowed neither learning nor good breeding on 'em.

Bellair

161He has been, as the sparkish word is, brisk upon the ladies already; he was yesterday at my Aunt Townley's, and gave Mrs. Loveit a catalogue of his good qualities under the character of a complete gentleman, who, according to Sir Fopling, ought to dress well, dance well, fence well, have a genius for love-letters, an agreeable voice for a chamber, be very amorous, something discreet, but not over-constant.

Medley

162Pretty ingredients to make an accomplished person.

Dorimant

163I am glad he pitched upon Loveit.

Bellair

164How so?

Dorimant

165I wanted a fop to lay to her charge, and this is as pat as may be.

Bellair

166I am confident she loves no man but you.

Dorimant

167The good fortune were enough to make me vain, but that I am in my nature modest.

Bellair

168Hark you, Dorimant; with your leave, Mr. Medley, 'tis only a secret concerning a fair lady.

Medley

169Your good breeding, sir, gives you too much trouble; you might have whispered without all this ceremony.

Bellair

170[to DORIMANT]. How stand your affairs with Belinda of late?

Dorimant

171She's a little jilting baggage.

Bellair

172Nay, I believe her false enough, but she's ne'er the worse for your purpose; she was with you yesterday in a disguise at the play.

Dorimant

173There we fell out, and resolved never to speak to one another more.

Bellair

174The occasion?

Dorimant

175Want of courage to meet me at the place appointed. These young women apprehend loving as much as the young men do fighting at first; but once entered, like them too, they all turn bullies straight.

Enter HANDY

Handy

176[to BELLAIR]. Sir, your man without desires to speak with you.

Bellair

177Gentlemen, I'll return immediately.

[Exit BELLAIR.

Medley

178A very pretty fellow this.

Dorimant

179He's handsome, well-bred, and by much the most tolerable of all the young men that do not abound in wit.

Medley

180Ever well-dressed, always complaisant, and seldom impertinent; you and he are grown very intimate, I see.

Dorimant

181It is our mutual interest to be so: it makes the women think the better of his understanding and judge more favourably of my reputation; it makes him pass upon some for a man of very good sense and I upon others for a very civil person.

Medley

182What was that whisper?

Dorimant

183A thing which he would fain have known, but I did not think it fit to tell him; it might have frighted him from his honourable intentions of marrying.

Medley

184Emilia, give her her due, has the best reputation of any young woman about the town who has beauty enough to provoke detraction; her carriage is unaffected, her discourse modest, not at all censorious nor pretending, like the counterfeits of the age.

Dorimant

185She's a discreet maid, and I believe nothing can corrupt her but a husband.

Medley

186A husband?

Dorimant

187Yes, a husband; I have known many women make a difficulty of losing a maidenhead who have afterwards made none of making a cuckold.

Medley

188This prudent consideration, I am apt to think, has made you confirm poor Bellair in the desperate resolution he has taken.

Dorimant

189Indeed, the little hope I found there was of her, in the state she was in, has made him by my advice contribute something towards the changing of her condition. Enter BELLAIR.
Dear Bellair, by heavens I thought we had lost thee; men in love are never to be reckoned on when we would form a company

Bellair

190Dorimant I am undone; my man has brought the most surprising news i' the world.

Dorimant

191Some strange misfortune is befallen your love.

Bellair

192My father came to town last night, and lodges i' the very house where Emilia lies.

Medley

193Does he know it is with her you are in love?

Bellair

194He knows I love, but knows not whom, without some officious sot has betrayed me.

Dorimant

195Your Aunt Townley is your confidante and favours the business.

Bellair

196I do not apprehend any ill office from her; I have received a letter, in which I am commanded by my father to meet him at my aunt's this afternoon; he tells me farther he has made a match for me, and bids me resolve to be obedient to his will or expect to be disinherited.

Medley

197Now's your time, Bellair; never had lover such an opportunity of giving a generous proof of his passion.

Bellair

198As how, I pray?

Medley

199Why, hang an estate, marry Emilia out of hand, and provoke your father to do what he threatens; 'tis but despising a coach, humbling yourself to a pair of goloshes, being out of countenance when you meet your friends, pointed at and pitied wherever you go by all the amorous fops that know you, and your fame will be immortal.

Bellair

200I could find in my heart to resolve not to marry at all.

Dorimant

201Fie, fie! that would spoil a good jest and disappoint the well-natured town of an occasion of laughing at you.

Bellair

202The storm I have so long expected hangs o'er my head and begins to pour down upon me; I am on the rack, and can have no rest till I'm satisfied in what I fear; where do you dine?

Dorimant

203At Long's or Locket's.

Medley

204At Long's let it be.

Bellair

205I'll run and see Emilia, and inform myself how matters stand; if my misfortunes are not so great as to make me unfit for company, I'll be with you.

[Exit BELLAIR.

Enter a Footman with a letter.

Footman

206[to DORIMANT]. Here's a letter, sir.

Dorimant

207The superscription's right: For Mr. Dorimant.

Medley

208Let's see: the very scrawl and spelling of a true-bred whore.

Dorimant

209I know the hand; the style is admirable, I assure you.

Medley

210Prithee read it.

Dorimant

211[reads]. "I told a you you dud not love me, if you dud, you would have seen me again e'er now; I have no money, and am very mallicolly; pray send me a guynie to see the operies. Your servant to command, Molly."

Medley

212Pray let the whore have a favourable answer, that she may spark it in a box and do honour to her profession.

Dorimant

213She shall, and perk up i' the face of quality. Is the coach at door?

Handy

214You did not bid me send for it.

Dorimant

215Eternal blockhead! [HANDY offers to go out.]
Hey, sot.

Handy

216Did you call me, sir?

Dorimant

217I hope you have not just exception to the name, sir?

Handy

218I have sense, sir.

Dorimant

219Not so much as a fly in winter. –How did you come, Medley?

Medley

220In a chair.

Footman

221You may have a hackney coach if you please, sir.

Dorimant

222I may ride the elephant if I please, sir; call another chair, and let my coach follow to Long's.

[Exeunt singing, Be calm, ye great parents, etc.

ACT II

SCENE I

Enter my Lady TOWNLEY and EMILIA.

Lady Townley

223I was afraid, Emilia, all had been discovered.

Emilia

224I tremble with the apprehension still.

Lady Townley

225That my brother should take lodgings i' the very house where you lie!

Emilia

226'Twas lucky we had timely notice to warn the people to be secret; he seems to be a mighty good-humoured old man.

Lady Townley

227He ever had a notable smirking way with him.

Emilia

228He calls me rogue, tells me he can't abide me, and does so bepat me.

Lady Townley

229On my word you are much in his favour then.

Emilia

230He has been very inquisitive, I am told, about my family, my reputation, and my fortune.

Lady Townley

231I am confident he does not i' the least suspect you are the woman his son's in love with.

Emilia

232What should make him then inform himself so particularly of me?

Lady Townley

233He was always of a very loving temper himself; it may be he has a doting fit upon him; who knows?

Emilia

234It cannot be.

Enter YOUNG BELLAIR.

Lady Townley

235Here comes my nephew. Where did you leave your father?

Young Bellair

236Writing a note within. Emilia, this early visit looks as if some kind jealousy would not let you rest at home.

Emilia

237The knowledge I have of my rival gives me a little cause to fear your constancy.

Young Bellair

238My constancy! I vow –

Emilia

239Do not vow –Our love is frail as is our life, and full as little in our power; and are you sure you shall outlive this day?

Young Bellair

240I am not; but when we are in perfect health 'twere an idle thing to fright ourselves with the thoughts of sudden death.

Lady Townley

241Pray what has passed between you and your father i' the garden?

Young Bellair

242He's firm in his resolution, tell me I must marry Mrs. Harriet, or swears he'll marry himself and disinherit me; when I saw I could not prevail with him to be more indulgent, I dissembled an obedience to his will which has composed his passion, and will give us time, and I hope opportunity, to deceive him.

Enter OLD BELLAIR with a note in his hand.

Lady Townley

243Peace, here he comes.

Old Bellair

244Harry, take this, and let your man carry it for me to Mr. Fourbes's chamber, my lawyer, i' the Temple. [Exit YOUNG BELLAIR.
[To EMILIA.] Neighbour, adod, I am glad to see thee here; make much of her, sister, she's one of the best of your acquaintance; I like her countenance and her behaviour well, she has a modesty that is not common i' this age, adod, she has.

Lady Townley

245I know her value, brother, and esteem her accordingly.

Old Bellair

246Advise her to wear a little more mirth in her face, adod, she's too serious.

Lady Townley

247The fault is very excusable in a young woman.

Old Bellair

248Nay, adod, I like her ne'er the worse, a melancholy beauty has her charms; I love a pretty sadness in a face which varies now and then, like changeable colours, into a smile.

Lady Townley

249Methinks you speak very feelingly, brother.

Old Bellair

250I am but five-and-fifty, sister, you know, an age not altogether insensible!
[To EMILIA.] Cheer up, sweetheart, I have a secret to tell thee may chance to make thee merry; we three will make collation together anon; i' the meantime mum, I can't abide you; go, I can't abide you. Enter YOUNG BELLAIR.
Harry, come, you must along with me to my Lady Woodvil's. I am going to slip the boy at a mistress.

Young Bellair

251At a wife, sir, you would say.

Old Bellair

252You need not look so glum, sir; a wife is no curse when she brings the blessings of a good estate with her; but an idle town flirt, with a painted face, a rotten reputation, and a crazy fortune, adod, is the devil and all; and such a one I hear you are in league with.

Young Bellair

253I cannot help detraction, sir.

Old Bellair

254Out, a pise o' their breeches, there are keeping fools enough for such flaunting baggages, and they are e'en too good for 'em.
[To EMILIA.] Remember night, go, you're a rogue you're a rogue; fare you well, fare you well; come, come, come along, sir.

[Exeunt OLD and YOUNG BELLAIR.

Lady Townley

255On my word the old man comes on apace; I'll lay my life he's smitten.

Emilia

256This is nothing but the pleasantness of his humour.

Lady Townley

257I know him better than you; let it work, it may prove lucky.

Enter a Page.

Page

258Madam, Mr. Medley has sent to know whether a visit will not be troublesome this afternoon?

Lady Townley

259[Exit Page. Send him word his visits never are so.

Emilia

260He's a very pleasant man.

Lady Townley

261He's a very necessary man among us women; he's not scandalous i' the least, perpetually contriving to bring good company together, and always ready to stop up a gap at ombre; then he knows all the little news o' the town.

Emilia

262I love to hear him talk o' the intrigues; let 'em be never so dull in themselves, he'll make 'em pleasant i' the relation.

Lady Townley

263But he improves things so much one can take no measure of the truth from him. Mr. Dorimant swears a flea or a maggot is not made more monstrous by a magnifying glass than a story is by his telling it.

Emilia

264Hold, here he comes.

Enter MEDLEY.

Lady Townley

265Mr. Medley.

Medley

266Your servant, madam.

Lady Townley

267You have made yourself a stranger of late.

Emilia

268I believe you took a surfeit of ombre last time you were here.

Medley

269Indeed I had my bellyful of that termagant Lady Dealer; there never was so insatiable a carder, an old gleeker never loved to sit to't like her; I have played with her now at least a dozen times till she's worn out all her fine complexion, and her tour would keep in curl no longer.

Lady Townley

270Blame her not, poor woman; she loves nothing so well as a black ace.

Medley

271The pleasure I have seen her in when she has had hope in drawing for a matadore!

Emilia

272'Tis as pretty sport to her as persuading masks off is to you to make discoveries.

Lady Townley

273Pray, where's your friend Mr. Dorimant?

Medley

274Soliciting his affairs; he's a man of great employment has more mistresses now depending than the most eminent lawyer in England has causes.

Emilia

275Here has been Mrs. Loveit, so uneasy and out of humour these two days.

Lady Townley

276How strangely love and jealousy rage in that poor woman!

Medley

277She could not have picked out a devil upon earth so proper to torment her; he has made her break a dozen or two of fans already, tear half a score points in pieces, and destroy hoods and knots without number.

Lady Townley

278We heard of a pleasant serenade he gave her t'other night.

Medley

279A Danish serenade, with kettledrums and trumpets.

Emilia

280Oh, barbarous!

Medley

281What, you are of the number of the ladies whose ears are grown so delicate since our operas, you can be charmed with nothing but flutes douces and French hautboys.

Emilia

282Leave your raillery, and tell us is there any new wit come forth, songs or novels?

Medley

283A very pretty piece of gallantry by an eminent author called The Diversions of Brussels; very necessary to be read by all old ladies who are desirous to improve themselves at questions and commands, blindman's buff, and the like fashionable recreations.

Emilia

284Oh, ridiculous!

Medley

285Then there is The Art of Affectation, written by a late beauty of quality, teaching you how to draw up your breasts, stretch up your neck, to thrust out your breech, to play with your head, to toss up your nose, to bite your lips, to turn up your eyes, to speak in a silly soft tone of a voice, and use all the foolish French words that will infallibly make your person and conversation charming, with a short apology at the latter end, in the behalf of young ladies who notoriously wash and paint, though they have naturally good complexions.

Emilia

286What a deal of stuff you tell us?

Medley

287Such as the town affords, madam. The Russians hearing the great respect we have for foreign dancing have lately sent over some of their best balladines, who are now practising a famous ballet, which will be suddenly danced at the Bear Garden.

Lady Townley

288Pray forbear your idle stories, and give us an account of the state of love as it now stands.

Medley

289Truly there has been some revolutions in those affairs, great chopping and changing among the old, and some new lovers, whom malice, indiscretion, and misfortune have luckily brought into play.

Lady Townley

290What think you of walking into the next room, and sitting down before you engage in this business?

Medley

291I wait upon you, and I hope (though women are commonly unreasonable) by the plenty of scandal I shall discover to give you very good content, ladies.

[Exeunt.

SCENE II

Enter Mrs. LOVEIT and PERT. Mrs. LOVEIT putting up a letter, then pulling out her pocket-glass, and looking in it.

Loveit

292Pert.

Pert

293Madam.

Loveit

294I hate myself. I look so ill to-day.

Pert

295Hate the wicked cause on't, that base man Mr. Dorimant, who makes you torment and vex yourself continually.

Loveit

296He is to blame, indeed.

Pert

297To blame to be two days without sending, writing, or coming near you, contrary to his oath and covenant! 'twas to much purpose to make him swear: I'll lay my life there's not an article but he has broken –talked to the vizards i' the pit; waited upon the ladies from the boxes to their coaches; gone behind the scenes and fawned upon those little insignificant creatures the players; 'tis impossible for a man of his inconstant temper to forbear, I'm sure.

Loveit

298I know he is a devil, but he has something of the angel yet undefaced in him, which makes him so charming and agreeable that I must love him be he never so wicked.

Pert

299I little thought, madam, to see your spirit tamed to this degree, who banished poor Mr. Lackwit but for taking up another lady's fan in your presence.

Loveit

300My knowing of such odious fools contributes to the making of me love Dorimant the better.

Pert

301Your knowing of Mr. Dorimant, in my mind, should rather make you hate all mankind.

Loveit

302So it does, besides himself.

Pert

303Pray, what excuse does he make in his letter?

Loveit

304He has had business.

Pert

305Business in general terms would not have been a current excuse for another; a modish man is always very busy when he is in pursuit of a new mistress.

Loveit

306Some fop has bribed you to rail at him; he had business, I will believe it, and will forgive him.

Pert

307You may forgive him anything, but I shall never forgive him his turning me into ridicule, as I hear he does.

Loveit

308I perceive you are of the number of those fools his wit has made his enemies.

Pert

309I am of the number of those he's pleased to rally, madam; and if we may believe Mr. Wagfan and Mr. Caperwell, he sometimes makes merry with yourself too among his laughing companions.

Loveit

310Blockheads are as malicious to witty men as ugly women are to the handsome; 'tis their interest, and they make it their business to defame 'em.

Pert

311I wish Mr. Dorimant would not make it his business to defame you.

Loveit

312Should he, I had rather be made infamous by him than owe my reputation to the dull discretion of those fops you talk of. Enter BELINDA.
Belinda!

[Running to her.

Belinda

313My dear.

Loveit

314You have been unkind of late.

Belinda

315Do not say unkind, say unhappy!

Loveit

316I could chide you; where have you been these two days?

Belinda

317Pity me rather, my dear, where I have been so tired with two or three country gentlewomen, whose conversation has been more insufferable than a country fiddle.

Loveit

318Are they relations?

Belinda

319No, Welsh acquaintance I made when I was last year at St. Winifred's; they have asked me a thousand questions of the modes and intrigues of the town, and I have told 'em almost as many things for news that hardly were so when their gowns were in fashion.

Loveit

320Provoking creatures, how could you endure 'em?

Belinda

321[aside] Now to carry on my plot; nothing but love could make me capable of so much falsehood; 'tis time to begin, lest Dorimant should come before her jealousy has stung her.
[Laughs, and then speaks on.] I was yesterday at a play with 'em, where I was fain to show 'em the living, as the man at Westminster does the dead; that is Mrs. Such-a-one, admired for her beauty; this is Mr. Such-a-one, cried up for a wit; that is sparkish Mr. Such-a-one, who keeps reverend Mrs. Such-a-one, and there sits fine Mrs. Such-a-one, who was lately cast off by my Lord Such-a-one.

Loveit

322Did you see Dorimant there?

Belinda

323I did, and imagine you were there with him and have no mind to own it.

Loveit

324What should make you think so?

Belinda

325A lady masked in a pretty déshabillé, whom Dorimant entertained with more respect than the gallants do a common vizard.

Loveit

326[aside.] Dorimant at the play entertaining a mask, of heavens!

Belinda

327[aside.] Good.

Loveit

328Did he stay all the while?

Belinda

329Till the play was done, and then led her out, which confirms me it was you.

Loveit

330Traitor!

Pert

331Now you may believe he had business, and you may forgive him too.

Loveit

332Ungrateful, perjured man!

Belinda

333You seem so much concerned, my dear, I fear I have told you unawares what I had better have concealed for your quiet.

Loveit

334What manner of shape had she?

Belinda

335Tall and slender, her motions were very genteel; certainly she must be some person of condition.

Loveit

336Shame and confusion be ever in her face when she shows it!

Belinda

337I should blame your discretion for loving that wild man, my dear; but they say he has a way so bewitching that few can defend their hearts who know him.

Loveit

338I will tear him from mine, or die i' the attempt.

Belinda

339Be more moderate.

Loveit

340Would I had daggers, darts, or poisoned arrows in my breast, so I could but remove the thoughts of him from thence!

Belinda

341Fie, fie! your transports are too violent, my dear. This may be but an accidental gallantry, and 'tis likely ended at her coach.

Pert

342Should it proceed farther, let your comfort be, the conduct Mr. Dorimant affects will quickly make you know your rival, ten to one let you see her ruined, her reputation exposed to the town; a happiness none will envy her but yourself, madam.

Loveit

343Whoe'er she be, all the harm I wish her is, may she love him as well as I do, and may he give her as much cause to hate him!

Pert

344Never doubt the latter end of your curse, madam.

Loveit

345May all the passions that are raised by neglected love, jealousy, indignation, spite, and thirst of revenge, eternally rage in her soul as they do now in mine!

[Walks up and down with a distracted air.

Enter a Page.

Page

346Madam, Mr. Dorimant.

Loveit

347I will not see him.

Page

348I told him you were within, madam.

Loveit

349Say you lied, say I'm busy, shut the door; say anything.

Page

350He's here, madam.

Enter DORIMANT.

Dorimant

351They taste of death who do at Heaven arrive,
But we this paradise approach alive.

[To LOVEIT.] What, dancing the galloping nag without a fiddle? [Offers to catch her by the hand; she flings away and walks on.
I fear this restlessness of the body, madam.
[Pursuing her.] proceeds from an unquietness of the mind. What unlucky accident puts you out of humour; a point ill washed, knots spoiled i' the making up, hair shaded awry, or some other little mistake in setting you in order?

Pert

352A trifle, I my opinion, sir, more inconsiderable than any you mention.

Dorimant

353Oh, Mrs. Pert, I never knew you sullen enough to be silent; come, let me know the business.

Pert

354The business, sir, is the business that has taken you up these two days; how have I seen you laugh at men of business, and now to become a man of business yourself!

Dorimant

355We are not masters of our own affections, our inclinations daily alter; now we love pleasure, and anon we shall dote on business: human frailty will have it so, and who can help it?

Loveit

356Faithless, inhuman, barbarous man! –

Dorimant

357Good, now the alarm strikes. –

Loveit

358Without sense of love, of honour, or of gratitude, tell me –for I will know– what devil, masked she was, you were with at the play yesterday?

Dorimant

359Faith, I resolved as much as you, but the devil was obstinate and would not tell me.

Loveit

360False in this as in your vows to me! you do know.

Dorimant

361The truth is, I did all I could to know.

Loveit

362And dare you own it to my face? Hell and furies!

[Tears her fan in pieces.

Dorimant

363Spare your fan, madam; you are growing hot, and will want it to cool you.

Loveit

364Horror and distraction seize you, sorrow and remorse gnaw your soul, and punish all your perjuries to me! –

[Weeps.

Dorimant

365So thunder breaks the cloud in twain,
And makes a passage for the rain.

[Turning to BELINDA. Belinda, you are the devil that have raised this storm; you were at the play yesterday, and have been making discoveries to your dear.

Belinda

366You're the most mistaken man i' the world.

Dorimant

367It must be so, and here I vow revenge; resolve to pursue and persecute you more impertinently than ever any loving fop did his mistress, hunt you i' the Park, trace you i' the Mall, dog you in every visit you make, haunt you at the plays and i' the drawing room, hang my nose in your neck, and talk to you whether you will or no, and ever look upon you with such dying eyes, till your friends grow jealous of me, send you out of town, and make the world suspect your reputation.
[In a lower voice. At my Lady Townley's when we go from hence.

[He looks kindly on BELINDA.

Belinda

368I'll meet you there.

Dorimant

369Enough.

Loveit

370Stand off, you sha' not stare upon her so.

[Pushing DORIMANT away.

Dorimant

371Good! There's one made jealous already.

Loveit

372Is this the constancy you vowed?

Dorimant

373Constancy at my years! 'tis not a virtue in season; you might as well expect the fruit the autumn ripens i' the spring.

Loveit

374Monstrous principle!

Dorimant

375Youth has a long journey to go, madam: should I have set up my rest at the first inn I lodged at, I should never have arrived at the happiness I now enjoy.

Loveit

376Dissembler, damned dissembler!

Dorimant

377I am so, I confess; good nature and good manners corrupt me. I am honest in my inclinations, and would not, were't not to avoid offence, make a lady a little in years believe I think her young, wilfully mistake art for nature, and seem as fond of a thing I am weary of as when I doted on't in earnest.

Loveit

378False man!

Dorimant

379True woman!

Loveit

380Now you begin to show yourself!

Dorimant

381Love gilds us over and makes us show fine things to one another for a time, but soon the gold wears off, and then again the native brass appears.

Loveit

382Think on your oaths, your vows and protestations, perjured man.

Dorimant

383I made 'em when I was in love.

Loveit

384And therefore ought they not to bind? Oh, impious!

Dorimant

385What we swear at such a time may be a certain proof of a present passion; but to say truth, in love there is no security to be given for the future.

Loveit

386Horrid and ungrateful, begone, and never see me more.

Dorimant

387I am not one of those troublesome coxcombs, who because they were once well received take the privilege to plague a woman with their love ever after; I shall obey you, madam, though I do myself some violence.

[He offers to go, and LOVEIT pulls him back.

Loveit

388Come back, you sha' not go. Could you have the ill-nature to offer it?

Dorimant

389When love grows diseased, the best thing we can do is to put it to a violent death; I cannot endure the torture of a lingering and consumptive passion.

Loveit

390Can you think mine sickly?

Dorimant

391Oh, 'tis desperately ill! What worse symptoms are there than your being always uneasy when I visit you, your picking quarrels with me on slight occasions, and in my absence kindly listening to the impertinences of every fashionable fool that talks to you?

Loveit

392What fashionable fool can you lay to my charge?

Dorimant

393Why, the every cock-fool of all those fools, Sir Fopling Flutter.

Loveit

394I never saw him in my life but once.

Dorimant

395The worse woman you, at first sight to put on all your charms, to entertain him with that softness in your voice and all that wanton kindness in your eyes you so notoriously affect when you design a conquest.

Loveit

396So damned a lie did never malice yet invent. Who told you this?

Dorimant

397No matter; that ever I should love a woman that can dote on a senseless caper, a tawdry French ribbon, and a formal cravat.

Loveit

398You make me mad.

Dorimant

399A guilty conscience may do much; go on, be the game-mistress o' the town, and enter all our young fops as fast as they come from travel.

Loveit

400Base and scurrilous!

Dorimant

401A fine mortifying reputation 'twill be for a woman of your pride, wit, and quality!

Loveit

402This jealousy's a mere pretence, a cursed trick of your own devising; I know you.

Dorimant

403Believe it, and all the ill of me you can: I would not have a woman have the least good thought of me that can think well of Fopling; farewell; fall to, and much good may [it] do you with your coxcomb.

Loveit

404Stay, oh! stay, and I will tell you all.

Dorimant

405I have been told too much already.

[Exit DORIMANT.

Loveit

406Call him again.

Pert

407E'en let him go, a fair riddance.

Loveit

408Run, I say; call him again. I will have him called.

Pert

409The devil should carry him away first, were it my concern.

[Exit PERT.

Belinda

410He's frightened me from the very thoughts of loving men; for heaven's sake, my dear, do not discover what I told you; I dread his tongue as much as you ought to have done his friendship.

Enter PERT.

Pert

411He's gone, madam.

Loveit

412Lightning blast him!

Pert

413When I told him you desired him to come back, he smiled, made a mouth at me, flung into his coach, and said –

Loveit

414What did he say?

Pert

415"Drive away"; and then repeated verses.

Loveit

416Would I had made a contract to be a witch, when first I entertained this greater devil, monster, barbarian; I could tear myself in pieces. Revenge, nothing but revenge can ease me: plague, war, famine, fire, all that can bring universal ruin and misery on mankind; with joy I'd perish to have you in my power but this moment

[Exit LOVEIT.

Pert

417Follow, madam; leave her not in this outrageous passion.

[PERT gathers up the things.

Belinda

418He's given me the proof which I desired of his love:
But 'tis a proof of his ill-nature too;
I wish I had not seen him use her so.
I sigh to think that Dorimant may be
One day as faithless and unkind to me.

[Exeunt.

ACT III

SCENE I. –

Lady WOODVIL'S Lodgings.

Enter HARRIET and BUSY her woman.

Busy

419Dear madam! Let me set that curl in order.

Harriet

420Let me alone, I will shake 'em all out of order.

Busy

421Will you never leave this wildness?

Harriet

422Torment me not.

Busy

423Look! there's a knot falling off.

Harriet

424Let it drop.

Busy

425But one pin, dear madam.

Harriet

426How do I daily suffer under thy officious fingers!

Busy

427Ah, the difference that is between you and my Lady Dapper! How uneasy she is if the least thing be amiss about her!

Harriet

428She is indeed most exact; nothing is ever wanting to make her ugliness remarkable.

Busy

429Jeering people say so.

Harriet

430Her powdering, painting, and her patching never fail in public to draw the tongues and eyes of all the men upon her.

Busy

431She is indeed a little too pretending.

Harriet

432That women should set up for beauty as much in spite of nature as some men have done for wit!

Busy

433I hope, without offence, one may endeavour to make oneself agreeable.

Harriet

434Not when 'tis impossible. Women then ought to be no more fond of dressing than fools should be of talking. Hoods and modesty, masks and silence, things that shadow and conceal: they should think of nothing else.

Busy

435Jesu! madam, what will your mother think is become of you? For heaven's sake, go in again.

Harriet

436I won't.

Busy

437This is the extravagant'st thing that ever you did in your life, to leave her and a gentleman who is to be your husband.

Harriet

438My husband! Hast thou so little wit to think I spoke what I meant when I overjoyed her in the country with a low curtsey and What you please, madam, I shall ever be obedient?

Busy

439Nay, I know not, you have so many fetches.

Harriet

440And this was one to get her up to London; nothing else, I assure thee.

Busy

441Well, the man, in my mind, is a fine man.

Harriet

442The man indeed wears his clothes fashionably, and has a pretty negligent way with him, very courtly and much affected; he bows, and talks, and smiles so agreeably as he thinks.

Busy

443I never saw anything so genteel.

Harriet

444Varnished over with good breeding many a blockhead makes a tolerable show.

Busy

445I wonder you do not like him.

Harriet

446I think I might be brought to endure him, and that is all a reasonable woman should expect in a husband; but there is duty i' the case –and like the haughty Merab,

I find much aversion in my stubborn mind,
Which is bred by being promised and design'd.

Busy

447I wish you do not design your own ruin! I partly guess your inclinations, madam, –that Mr. Dorimant –

Harriet

448Leave your prating, and sing some foolish song or other.

Busy

449I will; the song you love so well ever since you saw Mr. Dorimant.

When first Amintas charm'd my heart,
My heedless sheep began to stray;
The wolves soon stole the greatest part,
And all will now be made a prey.
Ah! let not love your thoughts possess,
'Tis fatal to al shepherdess;
The dang'rous passion you must shun,
Or else, like me, be quite undone.

Harriet

450Shall I be paid down by a covetous parent for a purchase? I need no land; no, I'll lay myself out all in love. It is decreed –

Enter YOUNG BELLAIR.

Young Bellair

451What generous resolution are you making, madam?

Harriet

452Only to be disobedient, sir.

Young Bellair

453Let me join hands with you in that.

Harriet

454With all my heart; I never thought I should have given you mine so willingly. Here I, Harriet –

Young Bellair

455And I, Harry –

Harriet

456Do solemnly protest –

Young Bellair

457And vow –

Harriet

458That I with you –

Young Bellair

459And I with you –

Both.

460Will never marry.

Harriet

461A match!

Young Bellair

462And no match! How do you like this indifference now?

Harriet

463You expect I should take it ill, I see.

Young Bellair

464'Tis not unnatural for you women to be a little angry [if] you miss a conquest, though you would slight the poor man were he in your power.

Harriet

465There are some, it may be, have an eye like Bartholomew, big enough for the whole fair, but I am not of the number, and you may keep your gingerbread: 'twill be more acceptable to the lady whose dear image it wears, sir.

Young Bellair

466I must confess, madam, you came a day after the fair.

Harriet

467You own then you are in love.

Young Bellair

468I do.

Harriet

469The confidence is generous, and in return I could almost find in my heart to let you know my inclinations.

Young Bellair

470Are you in love?

Harriet

471Yes, with this dear town, to that degree I can scarce endure the country in landscapes and in hangings.

Young Bellair

472What a dreadful thing 'twould be to be hurried back to Hampshire?

Harriet

473Ah! name it not!

Young Bellair

474As for us, I find we shall agree well enough! Would we could do something to deceive the grave people!

Harriet

475Could we delay their quick proceeding, 'twere well; a reprieve is a good step towards the getting of a pardon.

Young Bellair

476If we give over the game we are undone; what think you of playing it on booty?

Harriet

477What do you mean?

Young Bellair

478Pretend to be in love with one another; 'twill make some dilatory excuses we may feign pass the better.

Harriet

479Let us do't, if it be but for the dear pleasure of dissembling.

Young Bellair

480Can you play your part?

Harriet

481I know not what it is to love, but I have made pretty remarks by being now and then where lovers meet. Where did you leave their gravities?

Young Bellair

482I' th' next room; your mother was censuring our modern gallant.

Enter OLD BELLAIR and Lady WOODVIL.

Harriet

483Peace! Here they come, I will lean against this wall and look bashfully down upon my fan, while you like an amorous spark modishly entertain me.

Lady Woodvil

484Never go about to excuse 'em; come, come, it was not so when I was a young woman.

Old Bellair

485Adod, they're something disrespectful.

Lady Woodvil

486Quality was then considered, and not rallied by every fleering fellow.

Old Bellair

487Youth will have its jest, adod it will.

Lady Woodvil

488'Tis good breeding now to be civil to none but players and Exchange women; they are treated by 'em as much above their condition as others are below theirs.

Old Bellair

489Out, a pise on 'em! talk no more; the rogues ha got an ill habit of preferring beauty, no matter where they find it.

Lady Woodvil

490See your son and my daughter, they have improved their acquaintance since they were within.

Old Bellair

491Adod, methinks they have; let's keep back and observe.

Young Bellair

492Now for a look and gestures that may persuade 'em I am saying all the passionate things imaginable.

Harriet

493Your head a little more on one side, ease yourself on your left leg, and play with your right hand.

Young Bellair

494Thus, is it not?

Harriet

495Now set your right leg firm on the ground, adjust your belt, then look about you.

Young Bellair

496A little exercising will make me perfect.

Harriet

497Smile, and turn to me again very sparkish.

Young Bellair

498Will you take your turn and be instructed?

Harriet

499With all my heart.

Young Bellair

500At one motion play your fan, roll your eyes, and then settle a kind look upon me.

Harriet

501So.

Young Bellair

502Now spread your fan, look down upon it, and tell the sticks with a finger.

Harriet

503Very modish!

Young Bellair

504Clap your hand up to your bosom, hold down your gown; shrug a little, draw up your breasts, and let 'em fall again gently, with a sigh or two, etc.

Harriet

505By the good instructions you give, I suspect you for one of those malicious observers who watch people's eyes and from innocent looks make scandalous conclusions.

Young Bellair

506I know some, indeed, who, out of mere love to mischief, are as vigilant as jealousy itself, and will give you an account of every glance that passes at a play and i' th' circle.

Harriet

507'Twill not be amiss now to seem a little pleasant.

Young Bellair

508Clap your fan then in both your hands, snatch it to your mouth, smile, and with a lively motion fling your body a little forwards. So, –now spread it; fall back on the sudden, cover your face with it, and break out in to a loud laughter – take up! look grave, and fall a-fanning of yourself –admirably well acted.

Harriet

509I think I am pretty apt at these matters.

Old Bellair

510Adod, I like this well.

Lady Woodvil

511This promises something.

Old Bellair

512Come! there is love i' th' case, adod there is, or will be; what say you, young lady?

Harriet

513All in good time, sir; you expect we should fall to and love, as gamecocks fight, as soon as we are set together; adod, you're unreasonable!

Old Bellair

514Adod, sirrah, I like thy wit well.

Enter a Servant.

Servant

515The coach is at the door, madam.

Old Bellair

516Go, get you and take the air together.

Lady Woodvil

517Will not you go with us?

Old Bellair

518Out a pise. Adod, I ha' business and cannot. We shall meet at night at my sister Townley's.

Young Bellair

519[aside] He's going to Emilia. I overheard him talk of a collation.

[Exeunt.

SCENE II

Enter Lady TOWNLEY, EMILIA, and Mr. MEDLEY.

Lady Townley

520I pity the young lovers we last talked of; though, to say truth, their conduct has been so indiscreet they deserve to be unfortunate.

Medley

521You've had an exact account, from the great lady i' th' box down to the little orange-wench.

Emilia

522You're a living libel, a breathing lampoon; I wonder you are not torn in pieces.

Medley

523What think you of setting up an office of intelligence for these matters? The project may get money.

Lady Townley

524You would have great dealings with country ladies.

Medley

525More than Muddiman has with their husbands.

Enter BELINDA.

Lady Townley

526Belinda, what has been become of you? we have not seen you here of late with your friend Mrs. Loveit.

Belinda

527Dear creature, I left her but no so sadly afflicted.

Lady Townley

528With her old distemper, jealousy?

Medley

529Dorimant has played her some new prank.

Belinda

530Well, that Dorimant is certainly the worst man breathing.

Emilia

531I once thought so.

Belinda

532And do you not think so still?

Emilia

533No, indeed!

Belinda

534Oh, Jesu!

Emilia

535The town does him a great deal of injury, and I will never believe what it says of a man I do not know again, for his sake.

Belinda

536You make me wonder!

Lady Townley

537He's a very well-bred man.

Belinda

538But strangely ill-natured.

Emilia

539Then he's a very witty man.

Belinda

540But a man of no principles.

Medley

541Your man of principles is a very fine thing indeed!

Belinda

542To be preferred to men of parts by women who have regard to their reputation and quiet. Well, were I minded to play the fool, he should be the last man I'd think of.

Medley

543He has been the first in many lady's favours, though you are so severe, madam.

Lady Townley

544What he may be for a lover I know not, but he's a very pleasant acquaintance, I am sure.

Belinda

545Had you seen him use Mrs. Loveit as I have done, you would never endure him more.

Emilia

546What, he has quarrelled with her again?

Belinda

547Upon the slightest occasion; he's jealous of Sir Fopling.

Lady Townley

548She never saw him in her life but yesterday, and that was there.

Emilia

549On my conscience, he's the only man in town that's her aversion; how horribly out of humour she was all the while he talked to her!

Belinda

550And somebody has wickedly told him –

Emilia

551Here he comes.

Enter DORIMANT.

Medley

552Dorimant! you are luckily come to justify yourself – here's a lady –

Belinda

553Has a word or two to say to you from a disconsolate person.

Dorimant

554You tender your reputation too much, I know, madam, to whisper with me before this good company.

Belinda

555To serve Mrs. Loveit, I'll make a bold venture.

Dorimant

556Here's Medley, the very spirit of scandal.

Belinda

557No matter!

Emilia

558'Tis something you are unwilling to hear, Mr. Dorimant.

Lady Townley

559Tell him, Belinda, whether he will or no.

Belinda

560[aloud] Mrs. Loveit –

Dorimant

561Softly, these are laughers, you do not know 'em.

Belinda

562[to DORIMANT, apart] In a word, you've made me hate you, which I thought you never could have done.

Dorimant

563In obeying your commands.

Belinda

564'Twas a cruel part you played! how could you act it?

Dorimant

565Nothing is cruel to a man who could kill himself to please you; remember, five o'clock to-morrow morning.

Belinda

566I tremble when you name it.

Dorimant

567Be sure you come.

Belinda

568I sha' not.

Dorimant

569Swear you will.

Belinda

570I dare not.

Dorimant

571Swear, I say.

Belinda

572By my life! by all the happiness I hope for –

Dorimant

573You will.

Belinda

574I will.

Dorimant

575Kind.

Belinda

576I am glad I've sworn, I vow I think I should ha' failed you else!

Dorimant

577Surprisingly kind! In what temper did you leave Loveit?

Belinda

578Her raving was prettily over, and she began to be in a brave way of defying you and all your works. Where have you been since you went from thence?

Dorimant

579I looked in at the play.

Belinda

580I have promised, and must return to her again.

Dorimant

581Persuade her to walk in the Mall this evening.

Belinda

582She hates the place, and will not come.

Dorimant

583Do all you can to prevail with her.

Belinda

584For what purpose?

Dorimant

585Sir Fopling will be here anon; I'll prepare him to set upon her there before me.

Belinda

586You persecute her too much; but I'll do all you'll ha' me.

Dorimant

587[aloud] Tell her plainly, 'tis grown so dull a business I can drudge on no longer.

Emilia

588There are afflictions in love, Mr. Dorimant.

Dorimant

589You women make 'em, who are commonly as unreasonable in that as you are at play; without the advantage be on your side a man can never quietly give over when he's weary.

Medley

590If you would play without being obliged to complaisance, Dorimant, you should play in public places.

Dorimant

591Ordinaries were a very good thing for that, but gentlemen do not of late frequent 'em; the deep play is now in private houses.

[BELINDA offering to steal away.

Lady Townley

592Belinda, are you leaving us so soon?

Belinda

593I am to go to the Park with Mrs. Loveit, madam.

[Exit BELINDA.

Lady Townley

594This confidence will go nigh to spoil this young creature.

Medley

595'Twill do her good, madam. Young men who are brought up under practising lawyers prove the abler counsel when they come to be called to the Bar themselves.

Dorimant

596The town has been very favourable to you this afternoon, my Lady Townley; you use to have an embarrass of chairs and coaches at your door, an uproar of footmen in your hall, and a noise of fools above here.

Lady Townley

597Indeed my house is the general rendezvous, and, next to the playhouse, is the common refuge of all the young idle people.

Emilia

598Company is a very good thing, madam, but I wonder you do not love it a little more chosen.

Lady Townley

599'Tis good to have an universal taste; we should love wit, but for variety be able to divert ourselves with the extravagancies of those who want it.

Medley

600Fools will make you laugh.

Emilia

601For once or twice; but the repetition of their folly after a visit or two grows tedious and unsufferable.

Lady Townley

602You are a little too delicate, Emilia.

Enter a Page.

Page

603Sir Fopling Flutter, madam, desires to know if you are to be seen.

Lady Townley

604Here's the freshest fool in town, and one who has not cloyed you yet. Page!

Page

605Madam!

Lady Townley

606Desire him to walk up.

[Exit Page.

Dorimant

607Do not you fall on him, Medley, and snub him. Soothe him up in his extravagance; he will show the better.

Medley

608You know I have a natural indulgence for fools, and need not his caution, sir.

Enter SIR FOPLING FLUTTER, with his Page after him.

Sir Fopling

609Page, wait without. Madam, [To LADY TOWNLEY.] I kiss your hands. I see yesterday was nothing of chance; the belles assemblées form themselves here every day. [To EMILIA.], Lady, your servant. Dorimant, let me embrace thee; without lying, I have not met with any of my acquaintance who retain so much of Paris as thou dost –the very air thou hadst when the marquis mistook thee i' th' Tuileries, and cried, Hey! Chevalier! and then begged thy pardon.

Dorimant

610I would fain wear in fashion as long as I can, sir; 'tis a thing to be valued in men as well as baubles.

Sir Fopling

611Thou art a man of wit, and understandest the town; prithee let thee and I be intimate, there is no living without making some good man the confidant of our pleasures.

Dorimant

612'Tis true! but there is no man so improper for such a business as I am.

Sir Fopling

613Prithee, why hast thou so modest an opinion of thyself?

Dorimant

614Why, first, I could never keep a secret in my life, and then there is no charm so infallibly makes me fall in love with a woman as my knowing a friend loves her. I deal honestly with you.

Sir Fopling

615Thy humour's very gallant, or let me perish; I knew a French count so like thee.

Lady Townley

616Wit, I perceive, has more power over you than beauty, Sir Fopling, else you would not have let this lady stand so long neglected.

Sir Fopling

617[to EMILIA] A thousand pardons, madam; some civilities due, of course, upon the meeting a long absent friend. The éclat of so much beauty, I confess, ought to have charmed me sooner.

Emilia

618The brilliant of so much good language, sir, has much more power than the little beauty I can boast.

Sir Fopling

619I never saw anything prettier than this high work on your point d'Espagne. –

Emilia

620'Tis not so rich as point de Venise. –

Sir Fopling

621Not altogether, but looks cooler, and is more proper for the season. Dorimant, is not that Medley?

Dorimant

622The same, sir.

Sir Fopling

623Forgive me, sir; in this embarrass of civilities I could not come to have you in my arms sooner. You understand an equipage the best of any man in town, I hear.

Medley

624By my own you would not guess it.

Sir Fopling

625There are critics who do not write, sir.

Medley

626Our peevish poets will scarce allow it.

Sir Fopling

627Damn 'em, they'll allow no man wit who does not play the fool like themselves, and show it! Have you taken notice of the calèche I brought over?

Medley

628Oh, yes! 'T has quite another air than th' English makes.

Sir Fopling

629'Tis as easily known from an English tumbril as an Inns of Court man is from one of us.

Dorimant

630Truly, there is a bel-air in calèches as well as men.

Medley

631But there are few so delicate to observe it.

Sir Fopling

632The world is generally very grossier here, indeed.

Lady Townley

633Here's very fine.

Emilia

634Extreme proper.

Sir Fopling

635A slight suit I made to appear in at my first arrival, not worthy your consideration, ladies.

Dorimant

636The pantaloon is very well mounted.

Sir Fopling

637The tassels are new and pretty.

Medley

638I never saw a coat better cut.

Sir Fopling

639It makes me show long-waisted, and, I think, slender.

Dorimant

640That's the shape our ladies dote on.

Medley

641Your breech, though, is a handful too high in my eye, Sir Fopling.

Sir Fopling

642Peace, Medley; I have wished it lower a thousand times, but a pox on't, 'twill not be.

Lady Townley

643His gloves are well fringed, large and graceful.

Sir Fopling

644I was always eminent for being bien-gante.

Emilia

645He wears nothing but what are originals of the most famous hands in Paris.

Sir Fopling

646You are in the right, madam.

Lady Townley

647The suit?

Sir Fopling

648Barroy.

Emilia

649The garniture?

Sir Fopling

650Le Gras.

Medley

651The shoes?

Sir Fopling

652Piccat.

Dorimant

653The periwig?

Sir Fopling

654Chedreux.

Lady Town and Emilia

655The gloves?

Sir Fopling

656Orangerie: you know the smell, ladies. Dorimant, I could find in my heart for an amusement to have a gallantry with some of our English ladies.

Dorimant

657'Tis a thing no less necessary to confirm the reputation of your wit than a duel will be to satisfy the town of your courage.

Sir Fopling

658Here was a woman yesterday –

Dorimant

659Mistress Loveit.

Sir Fopling

660You have named her.

Dorimant

661You cannot pitch on a better for your purpose.

Sir Fopling

662Prithee, what is she?

Dorimant

663A person of quality, and one who has a rest of reputation enough to make the conquest considerable. Besides, I hear she likes you too.

Sir Fopling

664Methought she seemed, though, very reserved and uneasy all the time I entertained her.

Dorimant

665Grimace and affectation. You will see her i' th' Mall to-night.

Sir Fopling

666Prithee let thee and I take the air together.

Dorimant

667I am engaged to Medley, but I'll meet you at St. James's and give you some information upon the which you may regulate your proceedings.

Sir Fopling

668All the world will be in the Park to-night: ladies, 'twere pity to keep so much beauty longer within doors and rob the Ring of all those charms that should adorn it. –Hey, page! Enter Page, and goes out again.
[Exit SIR FOPLING. See that all my people be ready. Dorimant, au revoir!

Medley

669A fine mettled coxcomb.

Dorimant

670Brisk and insipid.

Medley

671Pert and dull.

Emilia

672However you despise him, gentlemen, I'll lay my life he passes for a wit with many.

Dorimant

673That may very well be; nature has her cheats, stums a brain, and puts sophisticate dulness often on the tasteless multitude for true wit and good-humour. Medley, come.

Medley

674I must go a little way, I will meet you i' the Mall.

Dorimant

675I'll walk through the garden thither. [To the Women.] We shall meet anon and bow.

Lady Townley

676Not to-night; we are engaged about a business the knowledge of which may make you laugh hereafter.

Medley

677Your servant, ladies.

Dorimant

678Au revoir! as Sir Fopling says.

[Exeunt MEDLEY and DORIMANT.

Lady Townley

679The old man will be here immediately.

Emilia

680Let's expect him i' th' garden.

Lady Townley

681Go, you are a rogue.

Emilia

682I can't abide you.

[Exeunt.

SCENE III. –

The Mall

Enter HARRIET and YOUNG BELLAIR, she pulling him.

Harriet

683Come along.

Young Bellair

684And leave your mother?

Harriet

685Busy will be sent with a hue and cry after us; but that's no matter.

Young Bellair

686'Twill look strangely in me.

Harriet

687She'll believe it a freak of mine and never blame your manners.

Young Bellair

688What reverend acquaintance is that she has met?

Harriet

689A fellow-beauty of the last King's time, though by the ruins you would hardly guess it.

[Exeunt.

Enter DORIMANT, and crosses the stage.

Enter YOUNG BELLAIR and HARRIET.

Young Bellair

690By this time your mother is in a fine taking.

Harriet

691If your friend Mr. Dorimant were but here now, that she might find me talking with him.

Young Bellair

692She does not know him, but dreads him, I hear, of all mankind.

Harriet

693She concludes if he does but speak to a woman she's undone; is on her kneed every day to pray Heav'n defend me from him.

Young Bellair

694You do not apprehend him so much as she does.

Harriet

695I never saw anything in him that was frightful.

Young Bellair

696On the contrary, have you not observed something extreme delightful in his wit and person?

Harriet

697He's agreeable and pleasant I must own, but he does so much affect being so, he displeases me.

Young Bellair

698Lord, madam, all he does and says is so easy and so natural.

Harriet

699Some men's verses seem so to the unskilful, but labour i' the one and affectation in the other to the judicious plainly appear.

Young Bellair

700I never heard him accused of affectation before.

Enter DORIMANT, and stares upon her.

Harriet

701It passes on the easy town, who are favourably pleased in him to call it humour.

[Exeunt YOUNG BELLAIR and HARRIET.

Dorimant

702'Tis she! it must be she, that lovely hair, that easy shape, those wanton eyes, and all those melting charms about her mouth which Medley spoke of; I'll follow the lottery, and put in for a prize with my friend Bellair.

[Exit DORIMANT repeating:

In love the victors from the vanquish'd fly:
They fly that wound, and they pursue that die.

Enter YOUNG BELLAIR and HARRIET, and after them DORIMANT, standing at a distance.

Young Bellair

703Most people prefer High Park to this place.

Harriet

704It has the better reputation, I confess; but I abominate the dull diversions there, the formal bows, the affected smiles, the silly by-words, and amorous tweers in passing; here one meets with a little conversations now and then.

Young Bellair

705These conversations have been fatal to some of your sex, madam.

Harriet

706It may be so; because some who want temper have been undone by gaming, must others who have it wholly deny themselves the pleasure of play?

Dorimant

707Trust me, it were unreasonable, madam.

[Coming up gently, and bowing to her.

Harriet

708Lord! who's this?

[She starts, and looks grave.

Young Bellair

709Dorimant.

Dorimant

710Is this the woman your father would have you marry?

Young Bellair

711It is.

Dorimant

712Her name?

Young Bellair

713Harriet.

Dorimant

714I am not mistaken, she's handsome.

Young Bellair

715Talk to her, her wit is better than her face; we were wishing for you but now.

Dorimant

716[to HARRIET] Overcast with seriousness o' the sudden! A thousand smiles were shining in that face but now; I never saw so quick a change of weather.

Harriet

717[aside] I feel as great a change within; but he shall never know it.

Dorimant

718You were talking of play, madam; pray what may be your stint?

Harriet

719A little harmless discourse in public walks, or at most a appointment in a box barefaced at the playhouse; you are for masks and private meetings where women engage for all they are worth, I hear.

Dorimant

720I have been used to deep play, but I can make one at small game when I like my gamester well.

Harriet

721And be so unconcerned you'll ha' no pleasure in't.

Dorimant

722Where there is a considerable sum to be won the hope of drawing people in makes every trifle considerable.

Harriet

723The sordidness of men's natures, I know, makes 'em willing to flatter and comply with the rich, though they are sure never to be the better for 'em.

Dorimant

724'Tis in their power to do us good, and we despair not but at some time or other they may be willing.

Harriet

725To men who have fared in this town like you, 'twould be a great mortification to live on hope; could you keep a Lent for a mistress?

Dorimant

726In expectation of a happy Easter, and though time be very precious, think forty days well lost to gain your favour.

Harriet

727Mr. Bellair! let us walk, 'tis time to leave him; men grow dull when they begin to be particular.

Dorimant

728You're mistaken, flattery will not ensue, though I know you're greedy of the praises of the whole Mall.

Harriet

729You do me wrong.

Dorimant

730I do not; as I followed you I observed how you were pleased when the fops cried: "She's handsome, very handsome, By God she is," and whispered aloud your name, the thousand several forms you put your face into; then, to make yourself more agreeable, how wantonly you played with your head, flung back your locks, and looked smilingly over your shoulder at 'em.

Harriet

731I do not begging the men's, as you do the ladies' good liking, with a sly softness in your looks and a gentle slowness in your bows as you pass by 'em –as thus, sir; –
[Acts him.] Is not this like you?

Enter Lady WOODVIL and BUSY.

Young Bellair

732Your mother, madam.

[Pulls HARRIET; she composes herself.

Lady Woodvil

733Ah, my dear child Harriet!

Busy

734Now she is so pleased with finding her again she cannot chide her.

Lady Woodvil

735Come away!

Dorimant

736'Tis now but high Mall, madam the most entertaining time of all the evening.

Harriet

737I would fain see that Dorimant, mother, you so cry out for a monster; he's in the Mall, I hear.

Lady Woodvil

738Come away then! the plague is here, and you should dread the infection.

Young Bellair

739You may be misinformed of the gentleman.

Lady Woodvil

740Oh, no! I hope you do not know him! He is the prince of all the devils in the town, delights in nothing but in rapes and riots.

Dorimant

741If you did but hear him speak, madam!

Lady Woodvil

742Oh! he has a tongue, they say, would tempt the angels to a second fall.

Enter SIR FOPLING with his Equipage, six Footmen and a Page.

Sir Fopling

743Hey, Champagne, Norman, La Rose, La Fleur, La Tour, La Verdure. Dorimant ! –

Lady Woodvil

744Here, here he is among this rout, he names him ; come away, Harriet, come away.

[Exeunt Lady WOODVIL, HARRIET, BUSY, and YOUNG BELLAIR.

Dorimant

745This fool's coming has spoiled all: she's gone, but she has left a pleasing image of herself behind that wanders in my soul –It must not settle there.

Sir Fopling
