746What reverie is this? Speak, man.

Dorimant

747Snatch'd from myself, how far behind
Already I behold the shore

Enter MEDLEY.

Medley

748Dorimant, a discovery! I met with Bellair.

Dorimant

749You can tell me no news, sir; I know all.

Medley

750How do you like the daughter?

Dorimant

751You never came so near truth in your life as you did in her description.

Medley

752What think you of the mother?

Dorimant

753Whatever I think of her, she thinks very well of me, I find.

Medley

754Did she know you?

Dorimant

755She did not; whether she does now or no, I know not. Here was a pleasant scene towards, when in came Sir Fopling, mustering up his equipage, and at the latter end named me and frighted her away.

Medley

756Loveit and Belinda are not far off, I saw 'em alight at St. James's.

Dorimant

757[whispers] Sir Fopling, hark you, a word or two. Look you do not want assurance.

Sir Fopling

758I never do on these occasions.

Dorimant

759Walk on, we must not be seen together; make your advantage of what I have told you; the next turn you will meet the lady.

Sir Fopling

760Hey –Follow me all.

[Exeunt Sir FOPLING and his Equipage.

Dorimant

761Medley, you shall see good sport anon between Loveit and this Fopling.

Medley

762I thought there was something toward by that whisper.

Dorimant

763You know a worthy principle of hers?

Medley

764Not to be so much as civil to a man who speaks to her in the presence of him she professes to love.

Dorimant

765I have encouraged Fopling to talk to her to-night.

Medley

766Now you are here she will go nigh to beat him.

Dorimant

767In the humour she's in, her love will make her do some very extravagant thing, doubtless.

Medley

768What was Belinda's business with you at my Lady Townley's?

Dorimant

769To get me to meet Loveit here in order to an éclaircissement. I made some difficulty of it, and have prepared this rencontre to make good my jealousy.

Medley

770Here they come!

Enter LOVEIT, BELINDA, and PERT.

Dorimant

771I'll meet her and provoke her with a deal of dumb civility in passing by, then turn short and be behind her when Sir Fopling sets upon her.

See how unregarded now
That piece of beauty passes.

[Exeunt DORIMANT and MEDLEY.

Belinda

772How wonderful respectfully he bowed!

Pert

773He's always over-mannerly when he has done a mischief.

Belinda

774Methought indeed at the same time he had a strange despising countenance.

Pert

775The unlucky look, he thinks, becomes him.

Belinda

776I was afraid you would have spoke to him, my dear.

Loveit

777I would have died first; he shall no more find me the loving fool he has done.

Belinda

778You love him still!

Loveit

779No.

Pert

780I wish you did not.

Loveit

781I do not, and I will have you think so. What made you hale me to this odious place, Belinda?

Belinda

782I hate to be hulched up in a coach; walking is much better.

Loveit

783Would we could meet Sir Fopling now!

Belinda

784Lord! would you not avoid him?

Loveit

785I would make him all the advances that may be.

Belinda

786That would confirm Dorimant's suspicion, my dear.

Loveit

787He is not jealous, but I will make him so, and be revenged a way he little thinks on.

Belinda

788[aside] If she should make him jealous, that may make him fond of her again: I must dissuade her from it. Lord! my dear, this will certainly make him hate you.

Loveit

789'Twill make him uneasy, though he does not care for me; I know the effects of jealousy on men of his proud temper.

Belinda

790'Tis a fantastic remedy, its operations are dangerous and uncertain.

Loveit

791'Tis the strongest cordial we can give to dying love, it often brings it back when there's no sign of life remaining. But I design not so much the reviving his, as my revenge.

Enter SIR FOPLING and his Equipage.

Sir Fopling

792Hey! bid the coachman send home four of his horses, and bring the coach to Whitehall; I'll walk over the Park – Madam, the honour of kissing your fair hands is a happiness I missed this afternoon at my Lady Townley's.

Loveit

793You were very obliging, Sir Fopling, the last time I saw you there.

Sir Fopling

794The preference was due to your wit and beauty. Madam, your servant; there never was so sweet an evening.

Belinda

795'T has drawn all the rabble of the town hither.

Sir Fopling

796'Tis pity there's not an order made that none but the beau monde should walk here.

Loveit

797'Twould add much to the beauty of the place. See what a sort of nasty fellows are coming. Enter three ill-fashioned Fellows, singing,
'Tis not for kisses alone, etc.

Loveit

798Fo! Their periwigs are scented with tobacco so strong –

Sir Fopling

799It overcomes our pulvillio – Methinks I smell the coffee-house they come from.

1 Man

800Dorimant's convenient, Madam Loveit.

2 Man

801I like the oily buttock with her.

3 Man

802What spruce prig is that?

1 Man

803A caravan lately come from Paris.

2 Man

804Peace, they smoke.
There's something else to be done, etc.

[All of them coughing; exeunt, singing.

Enter DORIMANT and MEDLEY.

Dorimant

805They're engaged.

Medley

806She entertains him as if she liked him.

Dorimant

807Let us go forward; seem earnest in discourse, and show ourselves. Then you shall see how she'll use him.

Belinda

808Yonder's Dorimant, my dear.

Loveit

809[aside] I see him, he comes insulting; but I will disappoint him in his expectation. [To Sir FOPLING.] I like this pretty nice humour of yours, Sir Fopling. With what a loathing eye he looked upon those fellows!

Sir Fopling

810I sat near one of 'em at a play to-day, and was almost poisoned with a pair of cordovan gloves he wears.

Loveit

811Oh! filthy cordovan, how I hate the smell!

[Laughs in a loud affected way.

Sir Fopling

812Did you observe, madam, how their cravats hung oose an inch from their neck, and what a frightful air it gave 'em?

Loveit

813Oh! I took particular notice of one that is always spruced up with a deal of dirty sky-coloured ribbon.

Belinda

814That's one of the walking flageolets who haunt the Mall o' nights.

Loveit

815Oh! I remember him; he's a hollow tooth enough to spoil the sweetness of an evening.

Sir Fopling

816I have seen the tallest walk the streets with a dainty pair of boxes neatly buckled on.

Loveit

817And a little footboy at his heels pocket-high, with a flat cap –a dirty face.

Sir Fopling

818And a snotty nose.

Loveit

819Oh –odious! there's many of my own sex with that Holborn equipage trig to Gray's Inn Walks, and now and then travel hither on a Sunday.

Medley

820She takes no notice of you.

Dorimant

821Damn her! I am jealous of a counterplot!

Loveit

822Your liveries are the finest, Sir Fopling. –Oh, that page! that page is the prettily'st dressed –They are all Frenchmen?

Sir Fopling

823There's one damned English blockhead among 'em, you may know him by his mien.

Loveit

824Oh! that's he, that's he! what do you call him?

Sir Fopling

825Hey! –I know not what to call him. –

Loveit

826What's your name?

Footman

827John Trott, madam!

Sir Fopling

828Oh, unsufferable! Trott, Trott, Trott! there's nothing so barbarous as the names of our English servants. What countryman are you, sirrah?

Footman

829Hampshire, sir.

Sir Fopling

830Then Hampshire be your name. Hey, Hampshire!

Loveit

831Oh, that sound! that sound becomes the mouth of a man of quality!

Medley

832Dorimant, you look a little bashful on the matter.

Dorimant

833She dissembles better than I thought she could have done.

Medley

834You have tempted her with too luscious a bait: she bites at the coxcomb.

Dorimant

835She cannot fall from loving me to that?

Medley

836You begin to be jealous in earnest.

Dorimant

837Of one I do not love?

Medley

838You did love her.

Dorimant

839The fit has long been over.

Medley

840But I have known men fall into dangerous relapses when they have found a woman inclining to another.

Dorimant

841[to himself] He guesses the secret of my heart! I am concerned, but dare not show it lest Belinda should mistrust all I have done to gain her.

Belinda

842[aside] I have watched his look, and find no alteration there: did he love her, some signs of jealousy would have appeared.

Dorimant

843I hope this happy evening, madam, has reconciled you to the scandalous Mall; we shall have you now hankering here again.

Loveit

844Sir Fopling, will you walk?

Sir Fopling

845I am all obedience, madam.

Loveit

846Come along then, and let's agree to be malicious on all the ill-fashioned things we meet.

Sir Fopling

847We'll make a critique on the whole Mall, madam.

Loveit

848Belinda, you shall engage –

Belinda

849To the reserve of our friends, my dear.

Loveit

850No, no exceptions –

Sir Fopling

851We'll sacrifice all to our diversion.

Loveit

852All –all –

Sir Fopling

853All.

Belinda

854All? Then let it be.

[Exeunt Sir FOPLING, LOVEIT, BELINDA, and PERT, laughing.

Medley

855Would you had brought some more of your friends, Dorimant, to have been witnesses of Sir Fopling's disgrace and your triumph.

Dorimant

856'Twere unreasonable to desire you not to laugh at me; but pray do not expose me to the town this day or two.

Medley

857By that time you hope to have regained your credit?

Dorimant

858I know she hates Fopling, and only makes use of him in hope to work me on again; had it not been for some powerful considerations which will be removed to-morrow morning, I had made her pluck off this mask and show the passion that lies panting under.

Enter a Footman.

Medley

859Here comes a man from Bellair, with news of your last adventure.

Dorimant

860I am glad he sent him. I long to know the consequence of our parting.

Footman

861Sir, my master desires you to come to my Lady Townley's presently, and bring Mr. Medley with you. My Lady Woodvil and her daughter are there.

Medley

862Then all's well, Dorimant.

Footman

863They have sent for the fiddles and mean to dance! He bid me tell you, sir, the old lady does not know you, and would have you own yourself to be Mr. Courtage. They are all prepared to receive you by that name.

Dorimant

864That foppish admirer of quality who flatters the very meat at honourable tables, and never offers love to a woman below a lady-grandmother.

Medley

865You know the character you are to act, I see.

Dorimant

866This is Harriet's contrivance –wild, witty, lovesome, beautiful and young –come along, Medley.

Medley

867This new woman would well supply the loss of Loveit.

Dorimant

868That business must not end so; before to-morrow's sun is set I will revenge and clear it:

And you and Loveit to her cost shall fins,
I fathom all the depths of womankind.

[Exeunt.

ACT IV

SCENE I. –

The scene opens with the fiddles playing a country dance.

Enter DORIMANT, Lady WOODVIL, YOUNG BELLAIR, and Mrs. HARRIET, OLD BELLAIR, and EMILIA, Mr. MEDLEY and Lady TOWNLEY, as having just ended the dance.

Old Bellair

869So, so, so, a smart bout, a very smart bout, adod!

Lady Townley

870How do you like Emilia's dancing, brother?

Old Bellair

871Not at all, not at all.

Lady Townley

872You speak not what you think, I am sure.

Old Bellair

873No matter for that; go, bid her dance no more, it don't become her, it don't become her, tell her I say so.
[Aside.] Adod, I love her.

Dorimant

874[to LADY WOODVIL] All people mingle nowadays, madam, and in public places women of quality have the least respect showed 'em.

Lady Woodvil

875I protest you say the truth, Mr. Courtage.

Dorimant

876Forms and ceremonies, the only things that uphold quality and greatness, are now shamefully laid aside and neglected.

Lady Woodvil

877Well! this is not the women's age, let 'em think what they will; lewdness is the business now, love was the business in my time.

Dorimant

878The women indeed are little beholding to the young men of this age; they're generally only dull admirers of themselves, and make their court to nothing but their periwigs and their cravats, and would be more concerned for the disordering of 'em, though on a good occasion, than a young maid would be for the tumbling of her head or handkercher.

Lady Woodvil

879I protest you hit 'em.

Dorimant

880They are very assiduous to show themselves at Court well dressed to the women of quality, but their business is with the stale mistresses of the town, who are prepared to receive their lazy addresses by industrious old lovers who have cast 'em off and made 'em easy.

Harriet

881He fits my mother's humour so well, a little more and she'll dance a kissing dance with him anon.

Medley

882Dutifully observed, madam.

Dorimant

883They pretend to be great critics in beauty; by their talk you would think they liked no face, and yet can dote on an ill one if it belong to a laundress or a tailor' daughter; they cry a woman's past her prime at twenty, decayed at four-and-twenty, old and unsufferable at thirty.

Lady Woodvil

884Unsufferable at thirty! That they are in the wrong, Mr. Courtage, at five-and-thirty there are living proofs enough to convince 'em.

Dorimant

885Ay, madam, there's Mrs. Setlooks, Mrs. Droplip, and my Lady Loud; show me among all our opening buds a face that promises so much beauty as the remains of theirs.

Lady Woodvil

886The depraved appetite of this vicious age tastes nothing but green fruit, and loathes it when 'tis kindly ripened.

Dorimant

887Else so many deserving women, madam, would not be so untimely neglected.

Lady Woodvil

888I protest, Mr. Courtage, a dozen such good men as you would be enough to atone for that wicked Dorimant and all the under-debauchees of the town. [HARRIET, EMILIA, YOUNG BELLAIR, MEDLEY, and Lady TOWNLEY break out into a laughter.
What's the matter there?

Medley

889A pleasant mistake, madam, that a lady has made, occasions a little laughter.

Old Bellair

890Come, come, you keep 'em idle, they are impatient till the fiddles play again.

Dorimant

891You are not weary, madam?

Lady Woodvil

892One dance more; I cannot refuse you, Mr. Courtage.

[They dance.

Emilia

893You are very active, sir.

[After the dance OLD BELLAIR singing and dancing up to EMILIA.

Old Bellair

894Adod, sirrah, when I was a young fellow I could ha' capered up to my woman's gorget.

Dorimant

895You are willing to rest yourself, madam?

Lady Townley

896We'll walk into my chamber and sit down.

Medley

897Leave us Mr. Courtage, he's a dancer, and the young ladies are not weary yet.

Lady Woodvil

898We'll send him out again.

Harriet

899If you do not quickly, I know where to send for Mr. Dorimant.

Lady Woodvil

900This girl's head, Mr. Courtage, is ever running on that wild fellow.

Dorimant

901'Tis well you have got her a good husband, madam; that will settle it.

[Exeunt Lady TOWNLEY, Lady WOODVIL, and DORIMANT.

Old Bellair

902[to EMILIA] Adod, sweetheart, be advised, and do not throw thyself away on a young idle fellow.

Emilia

903I have no such intention, sir.

Old Bellair

904Have a little patience, thou shalt have the man I spake of. Adod, he loves thee, and will make a good husband; but no words.

Emilia

905But, sir. –

Old Bellair

906No answer –out a pise! peace! and think on't.

Enter DORIMANT.

Dorimant

907Your company is desired within, sir.

Old Bellair

908I go, I go, good Mr. Courtage – [To EMILIA.] Fare you well; go, I'll see you no more.

Emilia

909What have I done, sir?

Old Bellair

910You are ugly, you are ugly; is she not, Mr. Courtage?

Emilia

911Better words, or I shan't abide you.

Old Bellair

912Out a pise –adod, what does she say? Hit her a pat for me there.

[Exit OLD BELLAIR.

Medley

913You have charms for the whole family.

Dorimant

914You'll spoil all with some unseasonable jest, Medley.

Medley

915You see I confine my tongue and am content to be a bare spectator, much contrary to my nature.

Emilia

916Methinks, Mr. Dorimant, my Lady Woodvil is a little fond of you.

Dorimant

917Would her daughter were!

Medley

918It may be you may find her so; try her, you have an opportunity.

Dorimant

919And I will not lose it. Bellair, here's a lady has something to say to you.

Young Bellair

920I wait upon her. Mr. Medley, we have both business with you.

Dorimant

921Get you all together then. [To HARRIET.] That demure curtsey is not amiss in jest, but do not think in earnest it becomes you.

Harriet

922Affectation is catching, I find; from your grave bow I got it.

Dorimant

923Where had you all that scorn and coldness in your look?

Harriet

924From nature, sir; pardon my want of art: I have not learnt those softness and languishing which now in faces are so much in fashion.

Dorimant

925You need 'em not; you have a sweetness of your own, if you would but calm your frowns and let it settle.

Harriet

926My eyes are wild and wandering like my passions, and cannot yet be tied to rules of charming.

Dorimant

927Women, indeed, have commonly a method of managing those messengers of love; now they will look as if they would kill, and anon they will look as if they were dying. They point and rebate their glances the better to invite us.

Harriet

928I like this variety well enough, but hate the set face that always looks as it would say, Come, love me –a woman who at plays makes the doux yeux to a whole audience and at home cannot forbear 'em to her monkey.

Dorimant

929Put on a gentle smile, and let me see how well it will become you.

Harriet

930I am sorry my face does not please you as it is, but I shall not be complaisant and change it.

Dorimant

931Though you are obstinate, I know 'tis capable of improvement, and shall do you justice, madam, if I chance to be at Court when the critics of the circle pass their judgment; for thither you must come.

Harriet

932And expect to be taken in pieces, have all my features examined, every motion censured, and on the whole be condemned to be put pretty, or a beauty of the lowest rate. What think you?

Dorimant

933The women, nay, the very lovers who belong to the drawing-room, will maliciously allow you more than that; they always grant what is apparent that they may the better be believed when they name concealed faults they cannot easily be disproved in.

Harriet

934Beauty runs as great a risk exposed at Court as wit does on the stage, where the ugly and the foolish all are free to censure.

Dorimant

935[aside] I love her, and dare not let her know it; I fear she has an ascendant o'er me, and may revenge the wrongs I have done her sex. [To her.] Think of making a party, madam, love will engage.

Harriet

936You make me start! I did not think to have heard of love from you.

Dorimant

937I never knew what 'twas to have a settled ague yet, but now and then have had irregular fits.

Harriet

938Take heed! sickness after long health is commonly more violent and dangerous.

Dorimant

939[aside] I have took the infection from her, and feel the disease now spreading in me –
[To her.] Is the name of love so frightful that you dare not stand it?

Harriet

940'Twill do little execution out of your mouth on me, I am sure.

Dorimant

941It has been fatal –

Harriet

942To some easy women, but we are not all born to one destiny; I was informed you use to laugh at love and not make it.

Dorimant

943The time has been, but now I must speak –

Harriet

944If it be on that idle subject, I will put on my serious look, turn my head carelessly from you, drop my lip, let my eyelids fall and hang half o'er my eyes –thus– while you buzz a speech of an hour long in my ear, and I answer never a word; why do you not begin?

Dorimant

945That the company may take notice how passionately I make advances of love, and how disdainfully you receive 'em.

Harriet

946When your love's grown strong enough to make you bear being laughed at, I'll give you leave to trouble me with it: till when, pray forbear, sir.

Enter Sir FOPLING and others in masks.

Dorimant

947What's here, masquerades?

Harriet

948I thought that foppery had been left off and people might have been in private with a fiddle.

Dorimant

949'Tis endeavoured to be kept on foot still by some who find themselves the more acceptable the less they are known.

Young Bellair

950This must be Sir Fopling.

Medley

951That extraordinary habit shows it.

Young Bellair

952What are the rest?

Medley

953A company of French rascals whom he picked up in Paris and has brought over to be his dancing equipage on these occasions. Make him own himself; a fool is very troublesome when he presumes he is incognito.

Sir Fopling

954[to HARRIET] Do you know me?

Harriet

955Ten to one but I guess at you.

Sir Fopling

956Are you women as fond of a vizard as we men are?

Harriet

957I am very fond of a vizard that covers a face I do not like, sir.

Young Bellair

958Here are no masks, you see, sir, but those which came with you; this was intended a private meeting, but because you look like a gentleman, if you will discover yourself, and we know you to be such, you shall be welcome.

Sir Fopling

959[pulling off his mask] Dear Bellair.

Medley

960Sir Fopling! how came you hither?

Sir Fopling

961Faith, as I was coming late from Whitehall, after the King's couchée, one of my people told me he had heard fiddles at my Lady Townley's, and –

Dorimant

962You need not say any more, sir.

Sir Fopling

963Dorimant, let me kiss thee.

Dorimant

964[whispers] Hark you, Sir Fopling.

Sir Fopling

965Enough, enough –Courtage. A pretty kind of young woman that, Medley; I observed her in the Mall; more éveillée than our English women commonly are; prithee, what is she?

Medley

966The most noted coquette in town; beware of her.

Sir Fopling

967Let her be what she will, I know how to take my measures; in Paris the mode is to flatter the prude, laugh at the faux-prude, make serious love to the demi-prude, and only rally with the coquette. Medley, what think you?

Medley

968That for all this smattering of the mathematics, you may be out in your judgment at tennis.

Sir Fopling

969What a coq-à-l'âne is this? I talk of women, and thou answer'st tennis.

Medley

970Mistakes will be for want of apprehension.

Sir Fopling

971I am very glad of the acquaintance I have with this family.

Medley

972My lady truly is a good woman.

Sir Fopling

973Ah! Dorimant –Courtage I would say –would thou hadst spent the last winter in Paris with me. When thou wert there La Corneus and Sallyes were the only habitudes we had; a comedian would have been a bonne fortune. No stranger ever passed his time so well as I did some months before I came over. I was well received in a dozen families where all the women of quality used to visit; I have intrigues to tell thee more pleasant than ever thou read'st in a novel.

Harriet

974Write 'em, sir, and oblige us women; our language wants such little stories.

Sir Fopling

975Writing, madam, 's a mechanic part of wit; a gentleman should never go beyond a song or a billet.

Harriet

976Bussy was a gentleman.

Sir Fopling

977Who, d'Ambois?

Medley

978Was there ever such a brisk blockhead?

Harriet

979Not d'Ambois, sir, but Rabutin –he who writ The Loves of France.

Sir Fopling

980That may be madam: many gentlemen do things that are below 'em. Damn your authors, Courtage; women are the prettiest things we can fool away our time with.

Harriet

981I hope ye have wearied yourself to-nigh at Court sir, and will not think of fooling with anybody here.

Sir Fopling

982I cannot complain of my fortune there, madam – Dorimant –

Dorimant

983Again!

Sir Fopling

984Courtage, a pox on't! I have something to tell thee. When I had made my court within, I came out and flung myself upon the mat under the state i' th' outward room i' th' midst of half a dozen beauties who were withdrawn to jeer among themselves, as they called it.

Dorimant

985Did you know 'em?

Sir Fopling

986Not one of 'em by heavens! not I. But they were all your friends.

Dorimant

987How are you sure of that?

Sir Fopling

988Why we laughed at all the town; spared nobody but yourself; they found me a man for their purpose.

Dorimant

989I know you are malicious to your power.

Sir Fopling

990And faith I had occasion to show it for I never saw more gaping fools at a ball or on a Birthday.

Dorimant

991You learned who the women were?

Sir Fopling

992No matter; they frequent the drawing-room.

Dorimant

993And entertain themselves pleasantly at the expense of all the fops who come there.

Sir Fopling

994That's their business; faith, I sifted 'em, and find they have a sort of wit among them –Ah! filthy.

[Pinches a tallow candle.

Dorimant

995Look, he has been pinching the tallow candle.

Sir Fopling

996How can you breathe in a room where there's grease frying? Dorimant, thou art intimate with my lady, advise her for her own sake, and the good company that comes hither, to burn wax lights.

Harriet

997What are these masquerades who stand so obsequiously at a distance?

Sir Fopling

998A set of balladins whom I picked out of the best in France, and brought over with a flutes douces or two, my servants; they shall entertain you.

Harriet

999I had rather see you dance yourself, Sir Fopling.

Sir Fopling

1000And I had rather do it –all the company knows it –but, madam –

Medley

1001Come, come, no excuses, Sir Fopling.

Sir Fopling

1002By heavens, Medley!

Medley

1003Like a woman, I find you must be struggled with before one brings you to what you desire.

Harriet

1004[aside] Can he dance?

Emilia

1005And fence and sing too, if you'll believe him.

Dorimant

1006He has no more excellence in his heels than in his head. He went to Paris a plain bashful English blockhead, and is returned a fine undertaking French fop.

Medley

1007I cannot prevail.

Sir Fopling

1008Do not think it want of complaisance, madam.

Harriet

1009You are too well bred to want that, Sir Fopling. I believe it want of power.

Sir Fopling

1010By heavens! and so it is. I have sat up so damned late and drunk so cursed hard since I came to this lewd town, that I am fit for nothing but low dancing now, a corant, bourée, or a menuet; but St. André tells me, if I will but be regular, in one month I shall rise again. Pox on this debauchery!

[Endeavours at a caper.

Emilia

1011I have heard your dancing much commended.

Sir Fopling

1012It had the good fortune to please in Paris. I was judged to rise within an inch as high as the basque, in an entry I danced there.

Harriet

1013I am mightily taken with this fool; let us sit. Here's a seat, Sir Fopling.

Sir Fopling

1014At your feet, madam; I can be nowhere so much at ease: by your leave, gown.

Harriet and Emilia

1015Ah! you'll spoil it.

Sir Fopling

1016No matter, my clothes are my creatures; I make 'em to make my court to you ladies. Hey, – [TROTT dances.
qu'on commence! To an English dancer English motions. I was forced to entertain this fellow, one of my set miscarrying – Oh, horrid! leave your damned manner of dancing, and put on the French air; have you not a pattern before you –pretty well! Imitation in time may bring him to something.

After the dance enter OLD BELLAIR, Lady WOODVIL, and Lady TOWNLEY.

Old Bellair

1017Hey, adod! what have we here, a mumming?

Lady Woodvil

1018Where's my daughter –Harriet?

Dorimant

1019Here, here, madam. I know not but under these disguises there may be dangerous sparks; I gave the young lady warning.

Lady Woodvil

1020Lord! I am so obliged to you, Mr. Courtage.

Harriet

1021Lord! how you admire this man.

Lady Woodvil

1022What have you to except against him?

Harriet

1023He's a fop.

Lady Woodvil

1024He's not a Dorimant, a wild extravagant fellow of the times.

Harriet

1025He's a man made up of forms and commonplaces sucked out of the remaining lees of the last age.

Lady Woodvil

1026He's so good a man, that were you not engaged –

Lady Townley

1027You'll have but little night to sleep in.

Lady Woodvil

1028Lord! 'tis perfect day –

Dorimant

1029[aside] The hour is almost come I appointed Belinda, and I am not so foppishly in love here to forget: I am flesh and blood yet.

Lady Townley

1030I am very sensible, madam.

Lady Woodvil

1031Lord, madam!

Harriet

1032Look, in what a struggle is my poor mother yonder?

Young Bellair

1033She has much ado to bring out the compliment.

Dorimant

1034She strains hard for it.

Harriet

1035See, see! her head tottering, her eyes staring, and her under lip trembling.

Dorimant

1036[aside] Now, now she's in the very convulsions of her civility. 'Sdeath, I shall lose Belinda. I must fright her hence; she'll be an hour in this fit of good manners else.
[To Lady WOODVIL] Do you not know Sir Fopling, madam?

Lady Woodvil

1037I have seen that face. Oh, Heav'n! 'tis the same we met in the Mall; how came he here?

Dorimant

1038A fiddle in this town is a kind of fop-call; no sooner it strikes up but the house is besieged with an army of masquerades straight.

Lady Woodvil

1039Lord! I tremble, Mr. Courtage; for certain Dorimant is in the company.

Dorimant

1040I cannot confidently say he is not; you had best begone. I will wait upon you; your daughter is in the hands of Mr. Bellair.

Lady Woodvil

1041I'll see her before me. Harriet, come away.

Young Bellair

1042Lights! lights!

Lady Townley

1043Light down there.

Old Bellair

1044Adod, it needs not –

Dorimant

1045Call my Lady Woodvil's coach to the door quickly.

[Exeunt DORIMANT and YOUNG BELLAIR, with the Ladies.

Old Bellair

1046Stay, Mr. Medley, let the young fellows do that duty; we will drink a glass of wine together. 'Tis good after dancing; what mumming spark is that?

Medley

1047He is not to be comprehended in few words.

Sir Fopling

1048Hey! La Tour.

Medley

1049Whither away, Sir Fopling?

Sir Fopling

1050I have business with Courtage –

Medley

1051He'll but put the ladies into their coach, and come up again.

Old Bellair

1052In the meantime I'll call for a bottle.

[Exit OLD BELLAIR.

Enter YOUNG BELLAIR.

Medley

1053Where's Dorimant?

Young Bellair

1054Stolen home; he has had business waiting for him there all this night, I believe, by an impatience I observed in him.

Medley

1055Very likely; 'tis but dissembling drunkenness, railing at his friends, and the kind soul will embrace the blessing and forget the tedious expectation.

Sir Fopling

1056I must speak with him before I sleep.

Young Bellair

1057Emilia and I are resolved on that business.

Medley

1058Peace, here's your father.

Enter OLD BELLAIR and Butler, with a bottle of wine.

Old Bellair

1059The women are all gone to bed. Fill, boy; Mr. Medley, begin a health.

Medley

1060[whispers] To Emilia.

Old Bellair

1061Out, a pise! she's a rogue, and I'll not pledge you.

Medley

1062I know you will.

Old Bellair

1063Adod, drink it then.

Sir Fopling

1064Let us have the new bachique.

Old Bellair

1065Adod, that is a hard word; what does it mean, sir'

Medley

1066A catch or drinking song.

Old Bellair

1067Let us have it then.

Sir Fopling

1068Fill the glasses round, and draw up in a body. Hey! music!

[All]

1069(They sing) The pleasures of love and the joys of good wine
To perfect our happiness wisely we join.
We to beauty all day
Give the sovereign sway,
And her favourite nymphs devoutly obey.
At the plays we are constantly making our court,
And when they are ended we follow the sport,
To the Mall and the Park,
Where we love till 'tis dark;
Then sparkling champagne
Puts an end to their reign;
It quickly recovers
Poor languishing lovers,
Makes us frolic and gay, and drowns all our sorrow;
But, alas! we relapse again on the morrow.
Let ev'ry man stand
With his glass in his hand,
And briskly discharge at the word of command.
Here's a health to all those
Whom to-night we depose:
Wine and beauty by turns great souls should inspire.
Present altogether, and now, boys, give fire!

Old Bellair

1070Adod, a pretty business, and very merry.

Sir Fopling

1071Hark you, Medley, let you and I take the fiddles, and go waken Dorimant.

Medley

1072We shall do him a courtesy, if it be as I guess. For after the fatigue of this night, he'll quickly have his bellyful, and be glad of an occasion to cry: Take away, Handy.

Young Bellair

1073I'll go with you, and there we'll consult about affairs, Medley.

Old Bellair

1074[looks at his watch] Adod, 'tis six o'clock.

Sir Fopling

1075Let's away then.

Old Bellair

1076Mr. Medley, my sister tells me you are an honest man, and, adod, I love you. Few words and hearty –that's the way with old Harry, old Harry.

Sir Fopling

1077Light your flambeaux. Hey!

Old Bellair

1078What does the man mean?

Medley

1079'Tis day, Sir Fopling.

Sir Fopling

1080No matter. Our serenade will look the greater.

[Exeunt omnes.

SCENE II. –

DORIMANT'S Lodging. A table, a candle, a toilett, etc.

HANDY tying up linen

Enter DORIMANT in his gown, and BELINDA.

Dorimant

1081Why will you be gone so soon?

Belinda

1082Why did you stay out so late?

Dorimant

1083Call a chair, Handy. [Exit HANDY.
What makes you tremble so?

Belinda

1084I have a thousand fears about me. Have I not been seen, think you?

Dorimant

1085By nobody but myself and trusty Handy.

Belinda

1086Where are all your people?

Dorimant

1087I have dispersed 'em on sleeveless errands. What does that sigh mean?

Belinda

1088Can you be so unkind to ask me? –Well– [Sighs.] were it to do again –

Dorimant

1089We should do it, should we not?

Belinda

1090I think we should; the wickeder man you to make me love so well. Will you be discreet now?

Dorimant

1091I will.

Belinda

1092You cannot.

Dorimant

1093Never doubt it.

Belinda

1094I will not expect it.

Dorimant

1095You do me wrong.

Belinda

1096You have no more power to keep the secret than I had not to trust you with it.

Dorimant

1097By all the joys I have had, and those you keep in store –

Belinda

1098You'll do for my sake what you never did before –

Dorimant

1099By that truth thou hast spoken, a wife shall sooner betray herself to her husband –

Belinda

1100Yet I had rather you should be false in this, than in another thing you promised me.

Dorimant

1101What's that?

Belinda

1102That you would never see Loveit more but in public places, in the Park, at Court, and plays.

Dorimant

1103'Tis not likely a man should be fond of seeing a damned old play when there is a new one acted.

Belinda

1104I dare not trust your promise.

Dorimant

1105You may.

Belinda

1106This does not satisfy me. You shall swear you never will see her more.

Dorimant

1107I will! A thousand oaths –By all–

Belinda

1108Hold –You shall not, now I think on't better.

Dorimant

1109I will swear.

Belinda

1110I shall grow jealous of the oath, and think I owe your truth to that, not to your love.

Dorimant

1111Then, by my love, no other oath I'll swear.

Enter HANDY.

Handy

1112Here's a chair.

Belinda

1113Let me go.

Dorimant

1114I cannot.

Belinda

1115Too willingly, I fear.

Dorimant

1116Too unkindly feared. When will you promise me again?

Belinda

1117Not this fortnight.

Dorimant

1118You will be better than your word.

Belinda

1119I think I shall. Will it not make you love me less? [Starting.] Hark! what fiddles are these?

[Fiddles without.

Dorimant

1120Look out Handy.

[Exit HANDY and returns.

Handy

1121Mr. Medley, Mr. Bellair, and Sir Fopling; they are coming up.

Dorimant

1122How got they in?

Handy

1123The door was open for the chair.

Belinda

1124Lord! let me fly –

Dorimant

1125Here, here, down the back stairs. I'll see you into your chair.

Belinda

1126No, no, stay and receive 'em, and be sure you keep your word and never see Loveit more: let it be a proof of your kindness.

Dorimant

1127It shall –Handy, direct, her. Everlasting love go along with thee.

[Kissing her hand.

[Exeunt BELINDA and HANDY.

Enter YOUNG BELLAIR, MEDLEY, and Sir FOPLING.

Young Bellair

1128Not a-bed yet!

Medley

1129You have had an irregular fit, Dorimant.

Dorimant

1130I have.

Young Bellair

1131And is it off already?

Dorimant

1132Nature has done her part, gentlemen; when she falls kindly to work, great cures are effected in little time, you know.

Sir Fopling

1133We thought there was a wench in the case by the chair that waited. Prithee make us a confidence.

Dorimant

1134Excuse me.

Sir Fopling

1135Le sage Dorimant! was she pretty ?

Dorimant

1136So pretty she may come to keep her coach and pay parish duties if the good humour of the age continue.

Medley

1137And be of the number of the ladies kept by public-spirited men for the good of the whole town.

Sir Fopling

1138Well said, Medley.

[Sir FOPLING dancing by himself.

Young Bellair

1139See, Sir Fopling dancing.

Dorimant

1140You are practising and have a mind to recover, I see.

Sir Fopling

1141Prithee, Dorimant, why hast not thou a glass hung up there? A room is the dullest thing without one.

Young Bellair

1142Here is company to entertain you.

Sir Fopling

1143But I mean in case of being alone. In a glass a man may entertain himself.

Dorimant

1144The shadow of himself indeed.

Sir Fopling

1145Correct the errors of his motions and his dress.

Medley

1146I find, Sir Fopling, in your solitude you remember the saying of the wise man, and study yourself.

Sir Fopling

1147'Tis the best diversion in our retirements. Dorimant, thou art a pretty fellow, and wear'st thy clothes well, but I never saw thee have a handsome cravat. Were they made up like mine, they'd give another air to thy face. Prithee let me send my man to dress thee but one day. By heavens! an Englishman cannot tie a ribbon.

Dorimant

1148They are something clumsy-fisted.

Sir Fopling

1149I have brought over the prettiest fellow that ever spread a toilet; he served some time under Merille, the greatest genie in the world for a valet-de-chambre.

Dorimant

1150What, he who formerly belonged to the Duke of Candale?

Sir Fopling

1151The same, and got him his immortal reputation.

Dorimant

1152You've a very fine brandenburgh on, Sir Fopling.

Sir Fopling

1153It serves to wrap me up after the fatigue of a ball.

Medley

1154I see you often in it, with your periwig tied up.

Sir Fopling

1155We should not always be in a set dress; 'tis more en cavalier to appear now and then in a déshabillé.

Medley

1156Pray how goes your business with Loveit?

Sir Fopling

1157You might have answered yourself in the Mall last night. Dorimant! did you not see the advances she made me? I have been endeavouring at a song.

Dorimant

1158Already!

Sir Fopling

1159'Tis my coup d'essai in English; I would fain have thy opinion of it.

Dorimant

1160Let's see it.

Sir Fopling

1161Hey, page! give me my song –Bellair, here, thou hast a pretty voice, sing it.

Young Bellair

1162Sing it yourself, Sir Fopling.

Sir Fopling

1163Excuse me.

Young Bellair

1164You learnt to sing in Paris.

Sir Fopling

1165I did, of Lambert, the greatest master in the world; but I have his own fault, a weak voice, and care not to sing out of a ruelle.

Dorimant

1166A ruelle is a pretty cage for a singing fop, indeed.

Young Bellair

1167(reads the song) How charming Phyllis is! how fair!
Ah, that she were as willing
To ease my wounded heart of care,
And make her eyes less killing!
I sigh! I sigh! I languish now,
And love will not let me rest;
I drive about the Park, and bow
Still as I meet my dearest.

Sir Fopling

1168Sing it, sing it, man; it goes to a pretty new tune, which I am confident was made by Baptiste.

Medley

1169Sing it yourself, Sir Fopling; he does not know the tune.

Sir Fopling

1170I'll venture.

[Sir FOPLING sings.

Dorimant

1171Ay, marry, now 'tis something. I shall not flatter you, Sir Fopling; there is not much thought in't, but 'tis passionate, and well turned.

Medley

1172After the French way.

Sir Fopling

1173That I aimed at. Does it not give you a lively image of the thing? Slap down goes the glass, and thus we are at it.

Dorimant

1174It does indeed. I perceive, Sir Fopling, you'll be the very head of the sparks who are lucky in compositions of this nature.

Enter Sir FOPLING's Footman.

Sir Fopling

1175La Tour, is the bath ready?

Footman

1176Yes, sir.

Sir Fopling

1177Adieu donc, mes chers.

[Exit Sir FOPLING.

Medley

1178When have you your revenge on Loveit, Dorimant ?

Dorimant

1179I will but change my linen, and about it.

Medley

1180The powerful considerations which hindered have been removed then?

Dorimant

1181Most luckily this morning; you must along with me, my reputation lies at stake there.

Medley

1182I am engaged to Bellair.

Dorimant

1183What's your business?

Medley

1184Ma-tri-mony, an't like you.

Dorimant

1185It does not, sir.

Young Bellair

1186It may in time, Dorimant; what think you of Mrs. Harriet?

Dorimant

1187What does she think of me?

Young Bellair

1188I am confident she loves you.

Dorimant

1189How does it appear?

Young Bellair

1190Why, she's never well but when she's talking of you; but then she finds all the faults in you she can. She laughs at all who commend you; but then she speaks ill of all who do not.

Dorimant

1191Women of her temper betray themselves by their over-cunning. I had once a growing love with a lady who would always quarrel with me when I came to see her, and yet was never quiet if I stayed a day from her.

Young Bellair

1192My father is in love with Emilia.

Dorimant

1193That is a good warrant for your proceedings: go on and prosper; I must to Loveit. Medley, I am sorry you cannot be a witness.

Medley

1194Make her meet Sir Fopling again in the same place, and use him ill before me.

Dorimant

1195That may be brought about, I think. I'll be at your aunt's anon, and give you joy, Mr. Bellair.

Young Bellair

1196You had not best think of Mrs. Harriet too much; without church security there's no taking up there.

Dorimant

1197I may fall into the snare too. But

The wise will find a difference in our fate;
You wed a woman, I a good estate.

[Exeunt.

SCENE III

Enter the Chair with BELINDA; the Men set it down and open it. BELINDA starting.

Belinda

1198[surprised] Lord! where am I? in the Mall? Whither have you brought me?

1 Chairman

1199You gave us no directions, madam.

Belinda

1200[aside] The fright I was in made me forget it.

1 Chairman

1201We use to carry a lady from the squire's hither.

Belinda

1202[aside] This is Loveit; I am undone if she sees me. Quickly carry me away.

1 Chairman

1203Whither, an't like your honour?

Belinda

1204Ask no questions.

Enter LOVEIT's Footman.

Footman

1205Have you seen my lady, madam?

Belinda

1206I am just come to wait upon her.

Footman

1207She will be glad to see you, madam. She sent me to you this morning to desire your company, and I was told you went out by five o'clock.

Belinda

1208[aside] More and more unlucky!

Footman

1209Will you walk in, madam?

Belinda

1210I'll discharge my chair and follow. Tell your mistress I am here. [Exeunt Footman. [Gives the Chairmen money.
Take this, and if ever you should be examined, be sure you say you took me up in the Strand, over against the Exchange, as you will answer it to Mr. Dorimant.

Chairmen

1211We will, an't like your honour.

[Exeunt Chairmen.

Belinda

1212Now to come off, I must on –

In confidence and lies some hope is left;
'Twere hard to be found out in the first theft.

[Exit.

ACT V

SCENE I

Enter Mistress LOVEIT and PERT, her woman.

Pert

1213Well, in my eyes Sir Fopling is no such despicable person.

Loveit

1214You are an excellent judge!

Pert

1215He's as handsome a man as Mr. Dorimant, and as great a gallant.

Loveit

1216Intolerable! is't not enough I submit to his impertinences, but I must be plagued with yours too?

Pert

1217Indeed, madam –

Loveit

1218'Tis false, mercenary malice –

Enter her Footman.

Footman

1219Mrs. Belinda, madam –

Loveit

1220What of her?

Footman

1221She's below.

Loveit

1222How came she?

Footman

1223In a chair; ambling Harry brought her.

Loveit

1224He bring her! His chair stands near Dorimant's door, and always brings me from thence –Run and ask him where he took her up; go, there is no truth in friendship neither. Women as well as men –all are false, or all are so to me at least.

Pert

1225You are jealous of her too.

Loveit

1226You had best tell her I am. 'Twill become the liberty you take of late. This fellow's bringing of her, her going out by five o'clock –I know not what to think. Enter BELINDA.
Belinda, you are grown an early riser, I hear.

Belinda

1227Do you not wonder, my dear, what made me abroad so soon?

Loveit

1228You do not use to be so.

Belinda

1229The country gentlewomen I told you of (Lord! they have the oddest diversions!) would never let me rest till I promised to go with them to the markets this morning to eat fruit and buy nosegays.

Loveit

1230Are they so fond of a filthy nosegay?

Belinda

1231They complain of the stinks of the town, and are never well but when they have their noses in one.

Loveit

1232These are essences and sweet waters.

Belinda

1233Oh! they cry out upon perfumes they are unwholesome; one of 'em was falling into a fit with the smell of these narolii.

Loveit

1234Methinks, in complaisance you should have had a nosegay too.

Belinda

1235Do you think, my dear, I could be so loathsome to trick myself up with carnations and stock gillyflowers? I begged their pardon, and told them I never wore anything but orange flowers and tuberose. That which made me willing to go was a strange desire I had to eat some fresh nectarines.

Loveit

1236And had you any?

Belinda

1237The best I ever tasted.

Loveit

1238Whence came you now?

Belinda

1239From their lodgings, where I crowded out of a coach, and took a chair to come and see you, my dear.

Loveit

1240Whither did you send for that chair?

Belinda

1241'Twas going by empty.

Loveit

1242Where do these country gentlewomen lodge, I pray?

Belinda

1243In the Strand, over against the Exchange.

Pert

1244That place is never without a nest of 'em; they are always as one goes by fleering in balconies or staring out of windows.

Enter Footman.

Loveit

1245[whispers to the Footman] Come hither.

Belinda

1246[aside] This fellow by her order has been questioning the chairmen –I threatened 'em with the name of Dorimant; if they should have told truth I am lost for ever.

Loveit

1247In the Strand, said you?

Footman

1248Yes, madam, over against the Exchange.

[Exit Footman.

Loveit

1249She's innocent, and I am much to blame.

Belinda

1250[aside] I am so frighted my countenance will betray me.

Loveit

1251Belinda! what makes you look so pale?

Belinda

1252Want of my usual rest, and jolting up and down so long in an odious hackney.

Enter Footman.

Footman

1253Madam, Mr. Dorimant!

Loveit

1254What makes him here?

Belinda

1255[aside] Then I am betrayed indeed; he's broke his word, and I love a man that does not care for me.

Loveit

1256Lord! you faint, Belinda.

Belinda

1257I think I shall; such an oppression here on the sudden.

Pert

1258She has eaten too much fruit, I warrant you.

Loveit

1259Not unlikely!

Pert

1260'Tis that lies heavy on her stomach.

Loveit

1261Have her into chamber, give her some surfeit water, and let her lie down a little.

Pert

1262Come, madam, I was a strange devourer of fruit when I was young, so ravenous –

[Exit BELINDA, PERT leading her off.

Loveit

1263Oh, that my love would be put calm awhile! that I might receive this man all the scorn and indignation he deserves.

Enter DORIMANT.

Dorimant

1264Now for a touch of Sir Fopling to being with. Hey – page –give positive order that none of my people stir –let the canaille wait as they should do: since noise and nonsense have such powerful charms,

I, that I may successful prove,
Transform myself to what you love.

Loveit

1265If that would do, you need not change from what you are; you can be vain and loud enough.

Dorimant

1266But not with so good a grace as Sir Fopling. Hey, Hampshire! –Oh! that sound! that sound becomes the mouth of a man of quality.

Loveit

1267Is there a thing so hateful as a senseless mimic?

Dorimant

1268He's a great grievance indeed to all who like yourself, madam, love to play the fool in quiet.

Loveit

1269A ridiculous animal who has more of the ape than the ape has of the man in him.

Dorimant

1270I have as mean an opinion of a sheer mimic as yourself; yet were he all ape I should prefer him to the gay, the giddy, brisk, insipid, noisy fool you dote on.

Loveit

1271Those noisy fools, however you despise 'em, have good qualities, which weigh more (or ought at least) with us women than all the pernicious wit you have to boast of.

Dorimant

1272That I may hereafter have a just value for their merit, pray do me the favour to name 'em.

Loveit

1273You'll despise 'em as the dull effects of ignorance and vanity, yet I care not if I mention some. First, they really admire us, while you at best but flatter us well.

Dorimant

1274Take heed! fools can dissemble too –

Loveit

1275They may, but not so artificially as you: there is no fear they should deceive us. Then they are assiduous, sir; they are ever offering us their service, and always waiting on our will.

Dorimant

1276You owe that to their excessive idleness; they know not how to entertain themselves at home, and find so little welcome abroad, they are fain to fly to you who countenance 'em as a refuge against the solitude they would be otherwise condemned to.

Loveit

1277Their conversation too diverts us better.

Dorimant

1278Playing with your fan, smelling to your gloves, commending your hair, and taking notice how 'tis cut and shaded after the new way.

Loveit

1279Were it sillier than you can make it, you must allow 'tis pleasanter to laugh at others than to be laughed at ourselves, though never so wittily. Then though they want skill to flatter us, they flatter themselves so well they save us the labour; we need not take that care and pains to satisfy 'em of our love, which we so often lose on you.

Dorimant

1280They commonly indeed believe too well of themselves, and always better of you than you deserve.

Loveit

1281You are in the right; they have an implicit faith in us which keeps 'em from prying narrowly into our secrets, and saves us the vexations trouble of clearing doubts which your subtle and causeless jealousies every moment raise.

Dorimant

1282There is an inbred falsehood in women which inclines 'em still to them whom they may most easily deceive.

Loveit

1283The man who loves above his quality does not suffer more from the insolent impertinence of his mistress than the woman who loves above her understanding does from the arrogant presumptions of her friend.

Dorimant

1284You mistake the use of fools: they are designed for properties, and not for friends. You have an indifferent stock of reputation left yet. Lose it all like a frank gamester on the square; 'twill then be time enough to turn rook and cheat it up again on a good substantial bubble.

Loveit

1285The old and the ill-favoured are only fit for properties indeed, but young and handsome fools have met with kinder fortunes.

Dorimant

1286They have, to the shame of your sex be it spoken; 'twas this, the thought of this, made me, by a timely jealousy, endeavour to prevent the good fortune you are providing for Sir Fopling – but against a woman's frailty all our care is vain.

Loveit

1287Had I not with a dear experience bought the knowledge of your falsehood, you might have fooled me yet. This is not the first jealousy you have feigned to make a quarrel with me and get a week to throw away on some such unknown inconsiderable slut as you have been lately lurking with at plays.

Dorimant

1288Women, when they would break off with a man, never want th' address to turn the fault on him.

Loveit

1289You take a pride of late in using of me ill, that the town may know the power you have over me, which now (as unreasonably as yourself) expects that I (do me all the injuries you can) must love you still.

Dorimant

1290I am so far from expecting that you should, I begin to think you never did love me.

Loveit

1291Would the memory of it were so wholly worn out in me that I did doubt it too! What made you come to disturb my growing quiet?

Dorimant

1292To give you joy of your growing infamy.

Loveit

1293Insupportable! insulting devil! this from you, the only author of my shame! This from another had been but justice, but from you 'tis a hellish and inhuman outrage. What have I done?

Dorimant

1294A thing that puts you below my scorn and makes my anger as ridiculous as you have made my love.

Loveit

1295I walked last night with Sir Fopling.

Dorimant

1296You did, madam, and you talked and laughed aloud, ha, ha, ha! –Oh! that laugh! that laugh becomes the confidence of a woman of quality.

Loveit

1297You, who have more pleasure in the ruin of a woman's reputation than in the endearments of her love, reproach me not with yourself, and I defy you to name the man can lay a blemish on my fame.

Dorimant

1298To be seen publicly so transported with the vain follies of that notorious fop, to me is an infamy below the sin of prostitution with another man.

Loveit

1299Rail on, I am satisfied in the justice of what I did; you had provoked me to't.

Dorimant

1300What I did was the effect of a passion whose extravagances you have been willing to forgive.

Loveit

1301And what I did was the effect of a passion you may forgive if you think fit.

Dorimant

1302Are you so indifferent grown?

Loveit

1303I am.

Dorimant

1304Nay! then 'tis time to part. I'll send you back your letters you have so often asked for. I have two or three of 'em about me.

Loveit

1305Give 'em me.

Dorimant

1306You snatch as if you thought I would not –there– and may the perjuries in 'em be mine if e'er I see you more.

[Offers to go, she catches him.

Loveit

1307Stay!

Dorimant

1308I will not.

Loveit

1309You shall.

Dorimant

1310What have you to say?

Loveit

1311I cannot speak it yet.

Dorimant

1312Something more in commendation of the fool. Death! I want patience, let me go.

Loveit

1313[aside] I cannot. I can sooner part with the limbs that hold him. I hate that nauseous fool, you know I do.

Dorimant

1314Was it the scandal you were fond of then?

Loveit

1315You'd raised my anger equal to my love, a thing you ne'er could do before, and in revenge I did –I know not what I did. –Would you would not think on't any more!

Dorimant

1316Should I be willing to forget it, I shall be daily minded of it, 'twill be a commonplace for all the town to laugh at me; and Medley, when he is rhetorically drunk, will ever be declaiming on it in my ears.

Loveit

1317'Twill be believed a jealous spite! Come, forget, it.

Dorimant

1318Let me consult my reputation; you are too careless of it.
[pauses.] You shall meet Sir Fopling in the Mall again to-night.

Loveit

1319What mean you?

Dorimant

1320I have thought on it, and you must: 'tis necessary to justify my love to the world; you can handle a coxcomb as he deserves when you are not out of humour, madam.

Loveit

1321Public satisfaction for the wrong I have done you! This is some new device to make me more ridiculous.

Dorimant

1322Hear me.

Loveit

1323I will not.

Dorimant

1324You will be persuaded.

Loveit

1325Never.

Dorimant

1326Are you so obstinate?

Loveit

1327Are you so base?

Dorimant

1328You will not satisfy my love?

Loveit

1329I would die to satisfy that, but I will not to save from a thousand racks do a shameless thing to please your vanity.

Dorimant

1330Farewell, false woman!

Loveit

1331Do! go!

Dorimant

1332You will call me back again.

Loveit

1333Exquisite fiend! I knew you came but to torment me.

Enter BELINDA and PERT.

Dorimant

1334[surprised] Belinda here!

Belinda

1335[aside] He starts and looks pale; the sight of me has touched his guilty soul.

Pert

1336'Twas but a qualm, as I said, a little indigestion; the surfeit water did it, madam, mixed with a little mirabilis.

Dorimant

1337I am confounded, and cannot guess how she came hither!

Loveit

1338'Tis your fortune, Belinda, ever to be here when I am abused by this prodigy of ill-nature.

Belinda

1339I am amazed to find him here! How has he the face to come near you?

Dorimant

1340[aside] Here is fine work towards! I never was at such a loss before.

Belinda

1341One who makes a public profession of breach of faith and ingratitude; I loathe the sight of him.

Dorimant

1342[aside] There is no remedy; I must submit to their tongues now, and some other time bring myself off as well as I can.

Belinda

1343Other men are wicked, but then they have some sense of shame: he is never well but when he triumphs, nay, glories to a woman's face in his villainies.

Loveit

1344You are in the right, Belinda; but methinks your kindness for me makes you concern yourself too much with him.

Belinda

1345It does indeed, my dear; his barbarous carriage to you yesterday made me hope you ne'er would see him more, and the very next day to find him here again provokes me strangely; but, because I know you love him, I have done.

Dorimant

1346You have reproached me handsomely, and I deserve it for coming hither; but –

Pert

1347You must expect it, sir; all women will hate you for my lady's sake.

Dorimant

1348[aside to BELINDA]. Nay, if she begins too, 'tis time to fly; I shall be scolded to death else. I am to blame in some circumstances, I confess; but as to the main, I am not so guilty as you imagine. I shall seek a more convenient time to clear myself.

Loveit

1349Do it now! what impediments are here?

Dorimant

1350I want time, and you want temper.

Loveit

1351These are weak pretences!

Dorimant

1352You were never more mistaken in your life, and so farewell.

[DORIMANT flings off.

Loveit

1353Call a footman, Pert, quickly; I will have him dogged.

Pert

1354I wish you would not for my quiet and your own.

Loveit

1355I'll find out the infamous cause of all our quarrels, pluck her mask off, and expose her barefaced to the world.

Belinda

1356[aside] Let me but escape this time I'll never venture more.

Loveit

1357Belinda! you shall go with me.

Belinda

1358I have such a heaviness hangs on me with what I did this morning, I would fain go home and sleep, my dear.

Loveit

1359Death and eternal darkness! I shall never sleep again. Raging fevers seize the world, and make mankind as restless all as I am!

[Exit LOVEIT.

Belinda

1360I knew him false, and helped to make him so. Was not her ruin enough to fright me from the danger? It should have been, but love can take no warning.

[Exit BELINDA.

SCENE II. –

Lady TOWNLEY'S house

Enter MEDLEY, YOUNG BELLAIR, Lady TOWNLEY, EMILIA, and Chaplain.

Medley

1361Bear up, Bellair, and do not let us see that repentance in thine we daily do in married faces.

Lady Townley

1362This wedding will strangely surprise my brother when he knows it.

Medley

1363Your nephew ought to conceal it for a time, madam, since marriage has lost its good name; prudent men seldom expose their own reputations till 'tis convenient to justify their wives.

Old Bellair

1364[without] Where are you all there? Out, adod, will nobody hear?

Lady Townley

1365My brother! quickly, Mr. Smirk, into this closet; you must not be seen yet.

[He goes into the closet.

Enter OLD BELLAIR and Lady TOWNLEY's Page.

Old Bellair

1366Desire Mr. Fourbes to walk into the lover parlour, I will be with him presently.
[To YOUNG BELLAIR.] Where have you been, sir, you could not wait on me to-day?

Young Bellair

1367About a business.

Old Bellair

1368Are you so good at business? Adod, I have a business too you shall despatch out of hand, sir. Send for a parson, sister; my Lady Woodvil and her daughter are coming.

Lady Townley

1369What need you huddle up things thus?

Old Bellair

1370Out a pise! youth is apt to play the fool, and 'tis not good it should be in their power.

Lady Townley

1371You need not fear your son.

Old Bellair

1372He's been idling this morning, and, adod, I do not like him.
[To EMILIA] How dost thou do, sweetheart?

Emilia

1373You are very severe, sir; married in such haste.

Old Bellair

1374Go to, thou'rt a rogue, and I will talk with thee anon. Here's my Lady Woodvil come. Enter Lady WOODVIL, HARRIET, and BUSY.
Welcome, madam; Mr. Fourbes is below with the writings.

Lady Woodvil

1375Let us down, and make an end then.

Old Bellair

1376Sister, show the way. [To YOUNG BELLAIR, who is talking to HARRIET.]
Harry, your business lies not there yet; excuse him till we have done, lady, and then, adod, he shall be for thee. Mr. Medley, we must trouble you to be a witness.

Medley

1377I luckily came for that purpose, sir.

[Exeunt OLD BELLAIR, MEDLEY, YOUNG BELLAIR, Lady TOWNLEY, and Lady WOODVIL.

Busy

1378What will you do, madam?

Harriet

1379Be carried back and mewed up in the country again, run away here, anything rather than be married to a man I do not care for –Dear Emilia, do thou advise me.

Emilia

1380Mr. Bellair is engaged, you know.

Harriet

1381I do; but know not what the fear of losing an estate may fright him to.

Emilia

1382In the desperate condition you are in you should consult with some judicious man; what think you of Mr. Dorimant?

Harriet

1383I do not think of him at all.

Busy

1384She thinks of nothing else, I am sure.

Emilia

1385How fond your mother was of Mr. Courtage!

Harriet

1386Because I contrived the mistake to make a little mirth you believe I like the man.

Emilia

1387Mr. Bellair believes you love him.

Harriet

1388Men are seldom in the right when they guess at a woman's mind; would she whom he loves loved him no better!

Busy

1389[aside] That's e'en well enough, on all conscience.

Emilia

1390Mr. Dorimant has a great deal of wit.

Harriet

1391And takes a great deal of pains to show it.

Emilia

1392He's extremely well-fashioned.

Harriet

1393Affectedly grave or ridiculous wild and apish.

Busy

1394You defend him still against your mother.

Harriet

1395I would not were he justly rallied, but I cannot hear any one undeservedly railed at.

Emilia

1396Has your woman learnt the song you were so taken with?

Harriet

1397I was fond of a new thing; 'tis dull at second hearing.

Emilia

1398Mr. Dorimant made it.

Busy

1399She knows it, madam, and has made me sing it at least a dozen times this morning.

Harriet

1400Thy tongue is as impertinent as thy fingers.

Emilia

1401You have provoked her.

Busy

1402'Tis but singing the song, and I shall appease her.

Emilia

1403Prithee do.

Harriet

1404She has a voice will grate your ears worse than a cat-call, and dresses so ill she's scarce fit to trick up a yeoman's daughter on a holiday.

Busy

1405[sings.] As Amoret with Phyllis sat
One evening on the plain,
And saw the charming Strephon wait
To tell the nymph his pain,
The threat'ning danger to remove
She whisper'd in her ear,
Ah, Phyllis! if you would not love,
This shepherd do not hear.
None ever had so strange an art
His passion to convey
Into a list'ning virgin's heart,
And steal her soul away.
Fly, fly betimes, for fear you give
Occasion for your fate.
In vain, said she, in vain I strive,
Alas! 'tis now too late.

Enter DORIMANT.

Dorimant

1406Music so softens and disarms the mind –

Harriet

1407That not one arrow does resistance find.

Dorimant

1408Let us make use of the lucky minute then.

Harriet

1409[aside, turning from DORIMANT]. My love springs with my blood into my face, I dare not look upon him yet.

Dorimant

1410What have we here, the picture of celebrated beauty giving audience in public to a declared lover?

Harriet

1411Play the dying fop and make the piece complete, sir.

Dorimant

1412What think you if the hint were well improved –the whole mystery of making love pleasantly designed and wrought in a suit of hangings?

Harriet

1413'Twere needless to execute fools in effigy who suffer daily in their own persons.

Dorimant

1414[aside to EMILIA] Mrs. Bride, for such I know this happy day has made you.

Emilia

1415Defer the formal joy you are to give me and mind your business with her.
[Aloud] Here are dreadful preparations, Mr. Dorimant, writings sealing, and a parson sent for.

Dorimant

1416To marry this lady?

Busy

1417Condemned she is, and what will become of her I know not, without you generously engage in a rescue.

Dorimant

1418In this sad condition, madam, I can do no less than offer you my service.

Harriet

1419The obligation is not great; you are the common sanctuary for all young women who run from their relations.

Dorimant

1420I have always my arms pen to receive the distressed. But I will open my heart, and receive you where none yet did ever enter: you have filled it with a secret, might I but let you know it –

Harriet

1421Do not speak it if you would have me believe it; your tongue is so famed for falsehood 'twill do the truth an injury.

[Turns away her head.

Dorimant

1422Turn not away then; but look on me and guess it.

Harriet

1423Did you not tell me there was no credit to be given to faces? that women nowadays have their passions as much at will as they have their complexions, and put on joy and sadness, scorn and kindness, with the same ease they do their paint and patches –Are they the only counterfeits?

Dorimant

1424You wrong your own while you suspect my eyes; by all the hope I have in you, the inimitable colour in your cheeks is not more free from art than are the sighs I offer.

Harriet

1425In men who have been long hardened in sin we have reason to mistrust the first signs of repentance.

Dorimant

1426The prospect of such a heaven will make me persevere and give you marks that are infallible.

Harriet

1427What are those?

Dorimant

1428I will renounce all the joys I have in friendship and in wine, sacrifice to you all the interest I have in other women –

Harriet

1429Hold! –though I wish you devout I would not have you turn fanatic –Could you neglect these awhile and make a journey into the country?

Dorimant

1430To be with you I could live there and never send one thought to London.

Harriet

1431Whate'er you say, I know all beyond High Park's a desert to you, and that no gallantry can draw you farther.

Dorimant

1432That has been the utmost limit of my love, but now my passion knows no bounds, and there's no measure to be taken of what I'll do for you from anything I ever did before.

Harriet

1433When I hear you talk thus in Hampshire I shall begin to think there may be some truth enlarged upon.

Dorimant

1434Is this all? –will you not promise me?–

Harriet

1435I hate to promise! What we do then is expected from us, and wants much of the welcome it finds when it surprises.

Dorimant

1436May I not hope?

Harriet

1437That depends on you and not on me; and 'tis to no purpose to forbid it.

[Turns to BUSY]

Busy

1438Faith, madam, now I perceive the gentleman loves you too; e'en let him know your mind, and torment yourselves no longer.

Harriet

1439Dost think I have no sense of modesty?

Busy

1440Think, if you lose this you may never have another opportunity.

Harriet

1441May he hate me –a curse that frights me when I speak it –if ever I do a thing against the rules of decency and honour!

Dorimant

1442[to EMILIA] I am beholding to you for your good intentions, madam.

Emilia

1443I thought the concealing of our marriage from her might have done you better service.

Dorimant

1444Try her again.

Emilia

1445What have you resolved, madam? The time draws near.

Harriet

1446To be obstinate, and protest against this marriage.

Enter Lady TOWNLEY in haste.

Lady Townley

1447[To EMILIA] Quickly, quickly, let Mr. Smirk out of the closet.

[SMIRK comes out of the closet.

Harriet

1448A parson! had you laid him in here?

Dorimant

1449I knew nothing of him.

Harriet

1450Should it appear you did, your opinion of my easiness may cost you dear.

Enter OLD BELLAIR, YOUNG BELLAIR, MEDLEY, and Lady WOODVIL.

Old Bellair

1451Out a pise! the canonical hour is almost past. Sister, is the man of God come?

Lady Townley

1452He waits your leisure.

Old Bellair

1453By your favour, sir. Adod, a pretty spruce fellow! what may we call him?

Lady Townley

1454Mr. Smirk, my Lady Biggot's chaplain.

Old Bellair

1455A wise woman! adod, she is. The man will serve for the flesh as well as the spirit. Please you, sir, to commission a young couple to go to bed together i' God's name? Harry.

Young Bellair

1456Here, sir.

Old Bellair

1457Out a pise! without your mistress in your hand!

Smirk

1458Is this the gentleman?

Old Bellair

1459Yes, sir.

Smirk

1460Are you not mistaken, sir?

Old Bellair

1461Adod, I think not, sir.

Smirk

1462Sure you are, sir.

Old Bellair

1463You look as if you would forbid the banns; Mr. Smirk, I hope you have no pretension to the lady?

Smirk

1464Wish him joy, sir! I have done him the good office to-day already.

Old Bellair

1465Out a pise! what do I hear?

Lady Townley

1466Never storm, brother, the truth is out.

Old Bellair

1467How say you, sir? is this your wedding-day?

Young Bellair

1468It is, sir.

Old Bellair

1469And, adod, it shall be mine too; give me thy hand, sweetheart.
[To EMILIA] What dost thou mean? give me thy hand, I say.

[EMILIA kneels, and YOUNG BELLAIR.

Lady Townley

1470Come, come, give her your blessings; this is the woman your son loved and is married to.

Old Bellair

1471Ha! cheated! cozened! and by your contrivance, sister!

Lady Townley

1472What would you do with her? She's a rogue and you can't abide her.

Medley

1473Shall I hit her a pat for you, sir?

Old Bellair

1474Adod, you are all rogues, and I never will forgive you.

Lady Townley

1475Whither! whither away?

Medley

1476Let him go and cool awhile.

Lady Woodvil

1477[to DORIMANT] Here's a business broke out now; Mr. Courtage, I am made a fine fool of.

Dorimant

1478You see the old gentleman knew nothing of it.

Lady Woodvil

1479I find he did not. I shall have some trick put upon me if I stay in this wicked town any longer. Harriet! dear child! where art thou? I'll into the country straight.

Old Bellair

1480Adod, madam, you shall hear me first.

Enter LOVEIT and BELINDA.

Loveit

1481Hither my man dogged him.

Belinda

1482Yonder he stands, my dear.

Loveit

1483I see him.
[Aside] And with him the face that has undone me! Oh, that I were but where I might throw out the anguish of my heart! here it must rage within and break it.

Lady Townley

1484Mrs. Loveit, are you afraid to come forward?

Loveit

1485I was amazed to see so much company here in a morning, the occasion sure is extraordinary.

Dorimant

1486[Aside] Loveit and Belinda! the devil owes me a shame to-day, and I think never will have done paying it.

Loveit

1487Married! dear Emilia! how am I transported with the news?

Harriet

1488[to DORIMANT] I little thought Emilia was the woman Mr. Bellair was in love with; I'll chide her for not trusting me with the secret.

Dorimant

1489How do you like Mrs. Loveit?

Harriet

1490She's a famed mistress of yours, I hear.

Dorimant

1491She has been on occasion.

Old Bellair

1492[to Lady WOODVIL] Adod, madam, I cannot help it.

Lady Woodvil

1493You need make no more apologies, sir.

Emilia

1494[to LOVEIT] The old gentleman's excusing himself to my Lady Woodvil.

Loveit

1495Ha, ha, ha! I never heard of anything so pleasant.

Harriet

1496[to DORIMANT] She's extremely overjoyed at something.

Dorimant

1497At nothing; she is one of those hoiting ladies who gaily fling themselves about and force a laugh when their aching hearts are full of discontent and malice.

Loveit

1498Oh, Heav'n! I was never so near killing myself with aughing. Mr. Dorimant, are you a brideman?

Lady Woodvil

1499Mr. Dorimant! Is this Mr. Dorimant, madam?

Loveit

1500If you doubt it, your daughter can resolve you, I suppose.

Lady Woodvil

1501I am cheated too, basely cheated.

Old Bellair

1502Out a pise! what's here? more knavery yet?

Lady Woodvil

1503Harriet! on my blessing, come away, I charge you.

Harriet

1504Dear mother, do but stay and hear me.

Lady Woodvil

1505I am betrayed, and thou art undone, I fear.

Harriet

1506Do not fear it. I have not, nor ever will do anything against my duty; believe me, dear mother, do.

Dorimant

1507[to LOVEIT] I had trusted you with this secret, but that I knew the violence of your nature would ruin my fortune, as now unluckily it has. I thank you, madam.

Loveit

1508She's an heiress, I know, and very rich.

Dorimant

1509To satisfy you I must give up my interest wholly to my love; had you been a reasonable woman, I might have secured 'em both and been happy.

Loveit

1510You might have trusted me with anything of this kind, you know you might. Why did you go under a wrong name?

Dorimant

1511The story is too long to tell you know –be satisfied, this is the business, this is the mask has kept me from you.

Belinda

1512[aside] He's tender of my honour, though he's cruel to my love.

Loveit

1513Was it no idle mistress then?

Dorimant

1514Believe me, a wife, to repair the ruins of my estate that needs it.

Loveit

1515The knowledge of this makes my grief hang lighter on my soul; but I shall never more be happy.

Dorimant

1516Belinda!

Belinda

1517Do not think of clearing yourself with me, it is impossible. Do all men break their words thus?

Dorimant

1518Th' extravagant words they speak in love; 'tis as unreasonable to expect we should perform all we promise then, as do all we threaten when we are angry. When I see you next –

Belinda

1519Take no notice of me, and I shall not hate you.

Dorimant

1520How came you to Mrs. Loveit?

Belinda

1521By a mistake the chairmen made for want of my giving them directions.

Dorimant

1522'Twas a pleasant one. We must meet again.

Belinda

1523Never.

Dorimant

1524Never?

Belinda

1525When we do, may I be as infamous as you are false.

Lady Townley

1526Men of Mr. Dorimant's character always suffer in the general opinion of the world.

Medley

1527You can make no judgment of a witty man from common fame, considering the prevailing faction, madam.

Old Bellair

1528Adod, he's in the right.

Medley

1529Besides, 'tis a common error among women to believe too well of them they know and too ill of them they don't.

Old Bellair

1530Adod, he observes well.

Lady Townley

1531Believe me, madam, you will find Mr. Dorimant as civil a gentleman as you thought Mr. Courtage.

Harriet

1532If you would but know him better –

Lady Woodvil

1533You have a mind to know him better; come away! You shall never see him more.

Harriet

1534Dear mother, stay!

Lady Woodvil

1535I wo' not be consenting to your ruin.

Harriet

1536Were my fortune in your power –

Lady Woodvil

1537Your person is.

Harriet

1538Could I be disobedient I might take it out of yours, and put it into his.

Lady Woodvil

1539'Tis that you would be at; you would marry this Dorimant?

Harriet

1540I cannot deny it; I would, and never will marry any other man.

Lady Woodvil

1541Is this the duty that you promised?

Harriet

1542But I will never marry him against your will –

Lady Woodvil

1543[aside] She knows the way to melt my heart.
[To HARRIET] Upon yourself light your undoing.

Medley

1544[to OLD BELLAIR] Come, sir, you have not the heart any longer to refuse your blessing.

Old Bellair

1545Adod, I ha' not –Rise, and God bless you both! Make much of her, Harry, she deserves thy kindness.
[To EMILIA] Adod, sirrah, I did not think it had been in thee.

Enter Sir FOPLING and his Page.

Sir Fopling

1546'Tis a damned windy day; hey, page? Is my periwig right?

Page

1547A little out of order, sir.

Sir Fopling

1548Pox o' this apartment! it wants an antechamber to adjust oneself in.
[To LOVEIT] Madam, I came from your house, and your servants directed me hither.

Loveit

1549I will give order hereafter they shall direct you better.

Sir Fopling

1550The great satisfaction I had in the Mall last night has given me much disquiet since.

Loveit

1551'Tis likely to give me more than I desire.

Sir Fopling

1552What the devil makes her so reserved? Am I guilty of an indiscretion, madam?

Loveit

1553You will be of a great one if you continue your mistake, sir.

Sir Fopling

1554Something puts you out of humour.

Loveit

1555The most foolish inconsiderable thing that ever did.

Sir Fopling

1556Is it in my power?

Loveit

1557To hang or drown it; do one of 'em, and trouble me no more.

Sir Fopling

1558So fière? Serviteur, madame. Medley, where's Dorimant?

Medley

1559Methinks the lady has not made you those advances to-day she did last night, Sir Fopling.

Sir Fopling

1560Prithee do not talk of her.

Medley

1561She would be a bonne fortune.

Sir Fopling

1562Not to me, at present.

Medley

1563How so?

Sir Fopling

1564An intrigue now would be put a temptation to me to throw away that vigour on one which I mean shall shortly make my court to the whole sex in a ballet.

Medley

1565Wisely considered, Sir Fopling.

Sir Fopling

1566No one woman is worth the loss of a cut in a caper.

Medley

1567Not when 'tis so universally designed.

Lady Woodvil

1568Mr. Dorimant, every one has spoke so much in your behalf that I can no longer doubt but I was in the wrong.

Loveit

1569There's nothing but falsehood and impertinence in this world; all men are villains or fools. Take example from my misfortunes, Belinda; if thou wouldst be happy, give thyself wholly up to goodness.

Harriet

1570[To LOVEIT] Mr. Dorimant has been your God Almighty long enough; 'tis time to think of another.

Loveit

1571Jeered by her! I will lock myself up in my house, and never see the world again.

Harriet

1572A nunnery is the more fashionable place for such a retreat, and has been the fatal consequence of many a belle passion.

Loveit

1573Hold, heart! till I get home; should I answer 'twould make her triumph greater.

[Is going out.

Dorimant

1574Your hand, Sir Fopling.

Sir Fopling

1575Shall I wait upon you, madam?

Loveit

1576Legion of fools, as many devils take thee!

[Exit LOVEIT.

Medley

1577Dorimant! I pronounce thy reputation clear and henceforward when I would know anything of woman, I will consult no other oracle.

Sir Fopling

1578Stark mad, by all that's handsome! Dorimant, thou hast engaged me in a pretty business.

Dorimant

1579I have not leisure now to talk about it.

Old Bellair

1580Out a pise! what does this Man of Mode do here again!

Lady Townley

1581He'll be an excellent entertainment within, brother, and is luckily come to raise the mirth of the company.

Lady Woodvil

1582Madam, I take my leave of you.

Lady Townley

1583What do you mean, madam?

Lady Woodvil

1584To go this afternoon part of my way to Hartley.

Old Bellair

1585Adod, you shall stay and dine first; come, we will all be good friends, and you shall give Mr. Dorimant leave to wait upon you and your daughter in the country.

Lady Woodvil

1586If his occasions bring him that way, I have now so good an opinion of him he shall be welcome.

Harriet

1587To a great rambling lone house that looks as it were not inhabited, the family's so small; there you'll find my mother, an old lame aunt, and myself, sir, perched up on chairs at a distance in a large parlour, sitting moping like three or four melancholy birds in a spacious volery. Does not this stagger your resolution?

Dorimant

1588Not at all, madam. The first time I saw you you left me with the pangs of love upon me, and this day my soul has quite given up her liberty.

Harriet

1589This is more dismal than the country, Emilia; pity me who am going to that sad place. Methinks I hear the hateful noise of rooks already –kaw, kaw, kaw. There's music in the worst cry in London, My dill and cucumbers to pickle.

Old Bellair

1590Sister, knowing of this matter, I hope you have provided us some good cheer.

Lady Townley

1591I have, brother, and the fiddles too.

Old Bellair

1592Let 'em strike up then; the young lady shall have a dance before she departs. [Dance] [After the dance]
So, now we'll in and make this an arrant wedding-day.

[To the pit]
